Resolution No. 05-09
Storage of Alcoholic Beverages on Masonic Property
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow Lodges to store alcoholic
beverages on Masonic property under certain safeguards and conditions.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, a Lodge may, under California Masonic Code Section
15050, serve alcoholic beverages under suitable conditions within its facilities;
and
WHEREAS, if such beverages are to be made available in appropriate
quantities, they must be obtained in advance, requiring storage until the time of
intended use; and
WHEREAS, it is inevitable that there will, from time to tome, be
surplus amounts of such beverages; and
WHEREAS, these must be stored somewhere if there is any reason to
believe that they could be used at a future event; and
WHEREAS, the packing, transporting, and storage of such beverages
elsewhere may cause an undue burden on the brother required to perform such
duties, and will also require them to be moved again; and
WHEREAS, the repeated multiple episodes of transportation of such
beverages carries the probability of loss due to breakage in such processes,
causing unnecessary waste.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 15050 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

194

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

§15050. ALCOHOLIC BEVERAGES.
Alcoholic beverages may be sold, served, and consumed on property owned by
or leased to a Masonic Lodge, Temple Association, or Masonic Organization
and at Masonic activities held elsewhere if the following requirements are
observed:
A.

Alcoholic beverages may not be sold, served, or consumed in a Lodge
room or in a room in which Masonic ceremonies are normally
conducted except when such room serves a dual purpose as a
ceremonial room and social room;

B.

A Masonic Lodge, Temple Association, or Masonic Organization may
not maintain, on a regular or permanent basis, a bar stocked with
alcoholic beverages, but may store alcoholic beverages elsewhere on
the property in a secured area to which unauthorized persons do not
have access, such as a locked cabinet or closet, to the extent permitted
by and in accordance with rules established by the property owner;

C.

The funds of a Masonic Lodge or Temple Association may not be used
to purchase alcoholic beverages;

D.

Alcoholic beverages may not be served, consumed, or sold during the
course of a meeting of a Masonic Lodge, Temple Association, or
Masonic Organization;

E.

A Masonic Lodge or Temple Association may not directly or indirectly
apply for, assume title to, or operate under any government license or
permit for the purpose of consuming, serving or selling alcoholic
beverages, whether in, on, or away from premises owned, leased, or
rented by either of them;

F.

A Masonic Lodge, Temple Association, or Masonic Organization may
not rent or lease property in which it has an ownership interest to
business establishments wherein alcoholic beverages are served,
consumed or sold unless such service, consumption, and sale are
incident to and not the primary purpose of the business; and

G.

Any sale, service, or consumption of alcoholic beverages pursuant to
this section must comply with all applicable laws of the State of
California.

If this resolution is adopted, the following change will be made to the
last line of the fourth paragraph of the interpretation of Section 15050 of the
California Masonic Code in the Appendix:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

195

Alcohol should not be stored elsewhere on Masonic property either. Alcoholic
beverages obtained in accordance with the provisions of the California Masonic
Code may, with the express consent of all Lodges and Temple Associations
involved, be stored in an appropriately secured location on Masonic property.

Report of the Committee on Jurisprudence:
This report is given to the Grand Lodge by its legal counsel, the
Committee on Jurisprudence. Therefore, the advice contained in this report is
protected by the attorney client privilege and should not be disclosed outside
this room.
The proponents of this resolution seek to allow Lodges to store
alcoholic beverages on Masonic property under certain circumstances. Our
current rules on the use of alcoholic beverages were crafted many years ago to
come within the licensing exception for private parties. Normally, when
alcoholic beverages are served in a public setting, a license is required from the
California Department of Alcoholic Beverage Control. Groups with these
licenses are subject to state regulation, including state requirements regarding
membership practices. For example, these rules could require a club holding a
liquor license to accept women as members. As a private association, our Grand
Lodge is not subject to these requirements unless our lodges obtain liquor
licenses. To protect our right to limit our membership to men, our liquor
regulations were designed so that lodges would not be required to hold a liquor
license in order to serve alcoholic beverages.
If Resolution 05-09 is adopted and any lodge avails itself of the right to
store alcoholic beverages on Masonic property, then we believe that the private
party exemption from licensure will no longer apply. Under Section 23399.1 of
the California Business and Professions Code, the private party exemption
applies only if alcoholic beverages are not stored on Masonic property. If this
resolution is adopted and alcoholic beverages are stored on Masonic property,
then the lodge would be required to hold a liquor license and would subject our
Grand Lodge to rules requiring us to expand our membership to permit the
admission of women. Such a change would likely result in other Masonic
jurisdictions withdrawing recognition of California because the General
Regulations of Masonry limit our fraternity to men only. Adoption of this
resolution would jeopardize our position in the Masonic world.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution 05-09 seek to modify our law to permit
the storage of alcoholic beverages on property owned or leased by a building
association or Masonic Lodge.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

196

Alcoholic beverages in California are strictly controlled by State Laws
which are enforced by State and Local law enforcement officers. Section 15050
of our Masonic Code was carefully written to accomplish the following:
1.

To permit a lodge to sponsor an event wherein the incidental
use of alcoholic beverages on lodge properties is allowed.

2.

To protect a lodge property from the requirements of State
licensing laws applicable to those who furnish, sell or serve
alcoholic beverages.

A close reading of section 15050 of our Masonic Code makes clear that
the lodge never owns the alcoholic beverages which may have been served at an
event, and the lodge’s funds are not used in purchasing any alcoholic beverages.
Further, excess ticket sales proceeds over the costs of the event cannot be given
to the lodge until all reimbursements have been made. Thus, a lodge is not in
the business of profiting from the sale of liquor. These conditions were
included to protect our lodges from the intrusion of the State Alcohol Beverage
Control Authority.
Storing and serving alcohol beverages is not like storing and serving
coffee. The storage and subsequent serving of alcohol on our properties will not
only compromise the narrow line which we now walk, but may bring our lodge
premises under the authority of the State Alcohol Beverage licensing laws,
requiring a State ABC license, often a City license, and subsequent inspections
of our lodges properties and financial records by state and local officers, entirely
at their pleasure. Furthermore, it may jeopardize our fraternal status and bring
us under the many controls that apply to all businesses in this state.
Though the proponents of this resolution intend it to make life less
complicated for those lodges who have chosen to have alcohol served at their
events, these changes, however, will create a status under State law which is
unacceptable from a fraternal point of view.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policies and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

197

2006 RESOLUTIONS
Resolution No. 06-01
Unmasonic Conduct
The proponents of this Resolution seek to make offenses committed against
Masonic affiliated organizations unmasonic conduct.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Section 32030 of the California Masonic Code says that
it is NOT UNMASONIC CONDUCT to violate the laws of other fraternal or
social organizations, including Masonic Organizations; and
WHEREAS, our obligations are directed to a brother or a lodge; and
WHEREAS, grievous offenses have been committed against Masonic
affiliated organizations and concordant bodies; and
WHEREAS, this type of offense has not been properly addressed in
the California Masonic Code; and
WHEREAS, Masonic law should be changed to address these types of
occurrences.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 32020 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§32020. UNMASONIC CONDUCT.
The following breaches of faith or moral turpitude constitute unmasonic conduct
sufficient to support the filing of charges against a Mason:
A.

Any violation, before or after initiation, of the criminal laws of the
United States or of any other governmental entity involving moral
turpitude;

B.

The following acts committed against a Brother Mason, or the wife, or
child, or widow of such a Brother:

C.

1.

Use of defamatory, vile, indecent, profane, abusive or
threatening language,

2.

Habitual intemperance and drunkenness,

3.

Personal violence,

4.

Falsehood, treachery and deceit, or

5.

Actions similar to those offenses listed herein;

The following acts committed against his Lodge or the Master thereof:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

198

D.

1.

Misrepresentation of any fact in an application for degrees,
affiliation or restoration,

2.

Declaration that he does not believe in a Supreme Being,

3.

Attempted resignation from or renunciation of Masonry,

4.

Visiting a clandestine Lodge or vouching for a member of a
clandestine Lodge,

5.

Disclosure of secret or confidential matters to a non-Mason or
by a member of a Lodge to a non-member Mason not
otherwise entitled to the same,

6.

Reducing secret work to writing or use of a cipher ritual
during a tiled meeting,

7.

Refusal to obey Master’s authority properly exercised,

8.

Refusal to sign Lodge By-laws,

9.

Improper use of ballot or objection
advancement without proper cause, or

10.

Actions similar to those offenses listed herein;

to

candidate’s

The following acts committed by a Mason against Grand Lodge or the
Grand Master:
1.

Refusal to obey an edict of the Grand Master,

2.

Electioneering as prohibited by this Code,

3.

Organizing or promoting as a Lodge function:
a.

A trip to another Jurisdiction for the purpose of
conferring a degree, without first obtaining a
dispensation, or

b.

An event in violation of the Masonic or civil laws of
this Jurisdiction,

4.

Membership in any organization advocating the violent
overthrow of the government or an organization whose tenets
are in conflict with the basic principles and tenets of Masonry,
or

5.

Actions similar to those offenses listed herein;

E.

Acts or actions in breach of the obligations of the degrees; or

F.

The use of the Masonic name or emblems for other than legitimate
Masonic purposes., or

G.

Acts or actions that cheat, wrong or defraud any Masonic Organization.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

199

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 32030 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§32030. NOT UNMASONIC CONDUCT.
An act does not constitute a Masonic offense that neither violates this Code nor
involves a breach of faith or moral turpitude.
The following do not constitute unmasonic conduct sufficient to support the
filing of charges against a Mason:
A.

A business dispute not involving fraud or one that involves the
innocent inability to pay a just debt as promised;

B.

Political differences and acts in respect thereto;

C.

A possible conflict of interest arising from a transaction between a
Mason in his private capacity and his Lodge or Temple Association
where a full disclosure of the possible conflict has been made prior to
the transaction; or.

D.

Actions allegedly violating the laws of other fraternal or social
organizations, including Masonic Organizations.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this resolution seek to modify sections 32020 and
32030 of the California Masonic Code, both of which define the term
“unmasonic conduct.”
The proponents contend that section 32030-D, which states that it is
“not unmasonic conduct to violate the laws of other fraternal or social
organizations, including Masonic organizations,” has contributed to grievous
offenses against Masonic affiliated organizations and concordant bodies.
This Resolution would eliminate section 32030-D in its entirety.
Elimination of section 32030-D, which protects Masons against charges of
unmasonic conduct, based on a simple violation of another organization’s laws,
would expose all California masons to accusations of unmasonic conduct from a
universe of organizations, should a member violate the laws of any organization,
whether or not it is masonically affiliated and whether or not the conduct
involves a breach of moral turpitude.
Further, this Resolution would add the following to section 32020,
defining unmasonic conduct as “actions that cheat, wrong or defraud any
Masonic affiliated or concordant body wherein membership is predicated upon
being a master mason.”

200

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Under this language, if a mason is accused of cheating, wronging or
defrauding another organization, at a level that does not constitute an act of
moral turpitude, he could suffer Masonic penalties. If his actions do amount to
moral turpitude, our laws already speak to that as unmasonic conduct.
With respect to “wronging” another organization, even if it is
masonically associated, is such a vague description as to invite nuisance
Masonic charges and create confusion. Certain actions may not be the right
thing to do, and thus are wrong, but do not rise to the level of unmasonic
conduct serious enough to support Masonic charges.
It has long been the policy of this Grand Lodge to clarify and simplify
its laws, rules and regulations. There even exists this year a committee report
proposing a re-write of our Code to simplify its use. This Resolution, if
adopted, would cause confusion in trying to understand the definition of
unmasonic conduct and could impose serious injustices to our members.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policies and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The Finance Committee estimates that if the present number of trials
were doubled by this legislation it would cost an additional $22,000 each year,
or an increase in $0.37 in the per capita. This increase includes additional staff
time in the Grand Lodge Office to process the trial records, to assist the
Chairman of the Trial Review Committee with the handling of trials, and an
additional day at the Annual Communication for the Trial Review Committee to
meet to review these additional trials. If the present number of trials were
increased, but not doubled, the cost would be proportionately less.

Report of the Committee on Trial Review:
This resolution would add to the specified acts of Unmasonic Conduct
in Section 32020 – G. “Acts or actions that cheat, wrong or defraud any
Masonic Organization.”
This amendment to the existing Code would expand the definition of
“unmasonic conduct” to include as victims such other Masonic organizations as
Eastern Star, Scottish Rite, Sciots, High Twelve, etc.
It is difficult to imagine any Mason who would condone a Brother who
cheated, wronged or defrauded any person or organization. Such acts certainly
constitute “unmasonic conduct” irrespective of the status or identity of the
victim.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

201

This resolution would amend Section 32030 which attempts to define
what is not unmasonic conduct by striking out D. “Actions allegedly violating
the laws of other fraternal or social organizations, including Masonic
Organizations.”
It is extremely rare to find a punitive statute which enumerates or
specifies acts which do not constitute a violation. There are an infinite number
of examples of what is not unmasonic conduct and any attempt to compile a
complete list would be impossible as well as undesireable.
This committee has already observed Masonic trials which resulted in a
finding of unmasonic conduct where the victim was not within the narrow
definition of this Code section. Therefore, a significant increase in trials is not
anticipated.
The Committee is in favor of Resolution 06-01.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 06-02
Research Lodge Officers
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow a Master Mason to be a Master,
Warden or Secretary of a Regular Lodge and a Research Lodge at the same
time.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, it is desirable to have dedicated Masons serve their
Regular Lodge and their Research Lodge to the best of their ability by holding
elective office in their Regular Lodge and a Research Lodge; and
WHEREAS, officers and voting members of Research Lodges hold
plural memberships in Regular Lodges of this Grand Jurisdiction and the
Research Lodge; and
WHEREAS, plural members may not be Master, Warden or Secretary,
or any combination thereof, of more than one of such Lodges at the same time,
thereby precluding a member from concurrently holding office as Master,
Warden or Secretary of a Regular Lodge and a Research Lodge, or any
combination thereof; and
WHEREAS, Research Lodges hold Stated Meetings at least once
quarterly, thereby mitigating the likelihood of a conflict of interest if an elective
officer of a Regular Lodge held an elective office in a Research Lodge; and

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

202

WHEREAS, the provisions of this Code dealing with the
qualifications of Master, Senior Warden and Junior Warden do not apply to the
officers of Research Lodges; and
WHEREAS, Research Lodges do not have power to confer degrees;
and
WHEREAS, no officer or former officer of a Research Lodge shall, by
reason of this position alone, be a member of Grand Lodge; and the title of Past
Master is not applicable to one who has served as Master of a Research Lodge
only.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 23020 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§23020. MULTIPLE.
Dual membership is membership in Lodges of different Grand Jurisdictions.
Plural membership is membership in two or more Lodges in the same Grand
Jurisdiction. A multiple membership is either a dual or plural membership.
This Grand Lodge recognizes and authorizes multiple membership under the
following conditions:
A.

A member of a Lodge of this Jurisdiction residing in the Jurisdiction of
another Grand Lodge which allows dual membership may affiliate with
one or more Lodges in that Jurisdiction while retaining his membership
in this Jurisdiction. He becomes a dual member.

B.

A member of a Lodge of any other Grand Jurisdiction which allows
dual membership may affiliate with one or more Lodges under this
Grand Lodge while retaining his membership in such other
Jurisdiction. He becomes a dual member.

C.

A member, in good standing, of a Lodge in this Jurisdiction may retain
such membership and affiliate with any one or more other Lodges
under this Grand Lodge or join in a petition for a Dispensation for a
new Lodge and may present a paid up dues receipt in lieu of a Dimit or
Request for Dimit. He becomes a plural member.

Upon election of a multiple member or the formation of a new Lodge containing
one or more multiple members, the Secretary shall immediately notify the Grand
Secretary and also every other Lodge in which each multiple member holds
membership. The Grand Secretary shall provide forms to cover this section.
A multiple member shall pay all dues required by each Lodge wherein
membership is held and shall have all the rights and privileges of membership in
each of the Lodges. except that However, except for Research Lodges, he shall
not be Master, Warden or Secretary, or any combination thereof, of more than
one of such Lodges at the same time. The burden of the relief of a multiple

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

203

member, his wife, widow and orphans shall rest on the Lodges in which he
holds membership in proportion to the length of membership in each.
A multiple member may receive a Certificate of Discontinuance of Multiple
Membership as provided in this Code if otherwise entitled to the same.
Suspension or expulsion of a multiple member by any of his Lodges for other
than non-payment of dues, shall forthwith suspend or expel his membership in
all Lodges in this Jurisdiction, and the Secretary of any Lodge in this
Jurisdiction suspending or expelling such member shall immediately notify the
Grand Secretary and also every other Lodge in which the multiple member
holds membership. Any member who has been suspended or expelled in any
other Grand Jurisdiction for other than non-payment of dues, and who is
subsequently restored to membership therein, may also be restored to
membership in this Jurisdiction in the same manner as is provided in this Code
for members suspended or expelled by a Lodge in this Jurisdiction initially.
Suspension by a Lodge in this Jurisdiction for non-payment of dues of a
multiple member shall automatically suspend his membership in all Lodges in
this Jurisdiction. The Secretary of any Lodge suspending such a member shall
immediately notify the Grand Secretary and also every other Lodge in which the
multiple member holds membership. Notification shall also be given in case of
reinstatement by a Lodge in this Jurisdiction which shall automatically restore
him to membership in every other Lodge of which he was a member in this
Jurisdiction, upon payment of any dues or the performance of any other
obligations required for restoration to membership.
The suspension shall become effective only after a 60 day prior Notice of Intent
to Suspend has been sent to every other Lodge in which the member holds
membership. During this 60 day period the other Lodge or Lodges may contact
the member in an effort to avoid suspension. If within the 60 day period the
member fails to obtain good standing in all Lodges involved, he shall be
suspended. The suspension shall automatically terminate his membership in all
Lodges in this Jurisdiction.
Suspension by a Lodge in any other Grand Jurisdiction for non-payment of dues
shall automatically suspend his membership in all Lodges in this Jurisdiction.
The suspension and any restoration shall become effective in the same manner
as provided in this section for members suspended by a Lodge in this
Jurisdiction.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Currently, the California Masonic Code permits a member to act as the
Master, Warden or Secretary of only one Lodge at any given time. The
proponents of this Resolution seek to amend California Masonic Code Section
23020, paragraph 4, to allow the Master, Wardens or Secretary of a regular

204

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lodge to act concurrently as the Master, Warden or Secretary of a Research
Lodge.
The Research Lodge is clearly a special classification of constituent
Lodge that bears little resemblance to a regular Lodge. Its function is one of
general service to the Craft in the field of Masonic education and information.
Their Stated Meetings are held at least once quarterly and they have no judicial
powers. They have no power to confer degrees. They are chartered only by
name and without a Lodge number. The Master and Wardens of a Research
Lodge are not members of Grand Lodge by reason of membership in a Research
Lodge alone, nor is the title of Past Master applied to one who has served as a
Master of a Research Lodge only. The provisions of the California Masonic
Code dealing with the qualifications of the Master and Wardens of a regular
Lodge do not apply to those same officers of Research Lodges.
The policy against permitting a member to serve as a senior officer in
two lodges at the same time is based on the possibility of a conflict of interest
arising therefrom. In particular, it would allow the dual officer to be a member
of Grand Lodge, with voting rights, from two lodges at the same time. This
conflict would not arise by virtue of one being a senior officer in a Research
Lodge while also acting as a senior officer in a regular lodge. Many members of
Grand Lodge have served as elected officers in their Lodges, as well as senior
officers in concordant bodies concurrently. In this sense, the Research Lodge is
more like a concordant body than it is like a constituent Lodge. No conflict of
interest or loyalties arise from being a senior officer in a regular lodge and
a being a senior officer in a Research Lodge concurrently.
There appears to be no reason to prohibit a member from holding the
position of Master, Warden or Secretary of a regular lodge while at the same
time holding the position of a Master, Warden or Secretary of a Research
Lodge.
This Resolution is consistent with the policies and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The Finance Committee has determined that there is no financial
impact on Grand Lodge caused by the possible adoption of this measure.

Resolution ADOPTED.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

205

Resolution No. 06-03
Lodge Formation
The proponents of this Resolution seek to reduce the minimum required
members for a new lodge form 25 to 15.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Grand Lodge historically chartered four or five lodges
annually and it has been more than a quarter century since our jurisdiction has
performed at this level; and
WHEREAS, the development of new lodges is parallel to the
prosperity and continuance of California Freemasonry. Cities absent of
Masonic lodges cannot raise members or Masonicly benefit the local population
in general; and
WHEREAS, of the 515 cities in California, 230 currently have no
Masonic presence; and
WHEREAS, there are 152 California cities with populations greater
than 20,000 and 36 California cities that have populations greater than 50,000
that are currently lacking a Masonic lodge; and
WHEREAS, in an era of declining membership it is necessary to take
steps to facilitate the chartering of new lodges by reducing the number of Master
Masons required to form a new lodge; and
WHEREAS, California Freemasonry will benefit from encouraging
special interest or “affinity” lodges that my have small memberships, composed
of Brethren from various backgrounds and interests such as: firefighters,
educators, servicemen, or other specific ethnic and/or cultural interests.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 14010 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§14010. PETITION FOR DISPENSATION.
Upon the petition of at least 25 15 and not more than 50 Master Masons in good
standing, the Grand Master may grant them a dispensation to open and hold a
Lodge at the place named therein, under such name as shall be approved by him,
with power to make Masons and receive members by affiliation. He shall
appoint the Master and Wardens of the new Lodge, provided that the Grand
Secretary has certified to the Grand Master that each signer on the petition has
presented either a:
A.

Dimit;

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

206

B.

Dues receipt accompanied by a certified statement from the Secretary
of his Lodge that a Dimit will be issued on notification that the
requested dispensation has been issued;

C.

Dues receipt from members of Lodges in this Jurisdiction accompanied
by either:

D.

1.

A demand for transfer of membership, or

2.

A statement of the petitioner’s desire to affiliate as a plural
member; or

Certificate of Good Standing and a statement of the petitioner’s desire
to affiliate as a dual member in the case of a member belonging in a
Jurisdiction which recognizes dual membership.

Whenever this Section requires a dues receipt be presented, a photocopy
thereof, duly certified by the Secretary of any Lodge in this Jurisdiction, may be
substituted.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change Section 14010 of the
California Masonic Code to require a minimum 15, instead of 25, Master
Masons as the minimum number necessary to petition the Grand Master for a
dispensation to open and hold a new Lodge.
The proponents state that very few Lodges have been constituted in the
past 25 years. This is true. In fact, there have been many consolidations, even
charters surrendered, during this period. In the areas of the state where this has
occurred, Brethren either have become active in the newly consolidated Lodge,
or became dormant members because of the distances they had to travel to
attend meetings. Many of these former Lodges were in the 230 California cities
that now have no Masonic presence.
While California currently requires no less than 25 Master Masons to
petition for a dispensation to form a Lodge, many other Jurisdictions require
less. For instance, Alaska requires only 15, North Carolina 20, Ohio 12, Maine
seven and our Mother Grand Lodge, the United Grand Lodge of England, only
requires seven. The Grand Lodge of California requires only 12 Brethren to
perform the ritual of degrees. Under this Resolution, the Grand Master would
still have the right and duty to ensure that any new lodge would be financially
viable.
Masonry is on the rise in California. While overall membership has
declined, the number of new petitions received has been increasing annually. It
is the policy of this Grand Lodge to facilitate membership, and this Resolution
would help accomplish that goal.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

207

This Resolution is consistent with the policies and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The cost for the creation of a new lodge includes the instituting of the
new lodge by the Grand Master, and the constituting of the new lodge by the
Grand Master after a charter has been approved. If this legislation makes it
easier to form a new lodge, it might be assumed that more new lodges would be
created, and thus there would be a need for more such ceremonies. We estimate
that each ceremony costs Grand Lodge approximately $2,000. Since two
ceremonies per lodge are required, each new lodge would cost Grand Lodge
$4,000. Thus a new lodge of 15 members would pay $412.50 at the current per
capita rate of $27.50. The $4,000 is a “one time” expense.

Resolution CARRIED OVER.
Resolution No. 06-04
Support for Masonic Information and Child ID Booths by the
California Masonic Foundation
The proponents of this Resolution seek to support for Masonic information &
child id booths by the California Masonic Foundation.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Section 390 of the California Masonic Code presently
requires the Grand Lodge to sponsor information booths at the State Fair in
Sacramento; and
WHEREAS, these booths presently incorporate a public service
through the Child ID Program; and
WHEREAS, the Child ID Program is funded through the California
Masonic Foundation; and
WHEREAS, the California State Fair primarily serves only the
Sacramento area; and
WHEREAS, it would be more effective if the resources of Grand
Lodge and the California Masonic Foundation were available to other locations
in California.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 390 of the
California Masonic Code which reads as follows be repealed:

208

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

§390. INFORMATION BOOTHS.
Grand Lodge shall annually sponsor information booths at the State Fair in
California.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that a new Section 11050 be added
to the California Masonic Code as follows:

§11050. MASONIC INFORMATION AND CHILD ID BOOTHS AT
COMMUNITY AND COUNTY FAIRS AND AT THE CALIFORNIA
STATE FAIR
The California Masonic Foundation is authorized to provide for one or more
information booths and Child ID Program booths at community and county
fairs, and at the California State Fair, as it may find appropriate. The
Foundation is also authorized to establish rules and regulations for such booths,
including requirements for local participation and matching funds as it may set
forth in applications for funding. Such applications shall also ensure proper
local sponsorship, including staffing of the booths. All such booths shall be
under the control of the Foundation as long as any funding from the Foundation
is involved, and the Foundation shall annually determine the level of funding
available for these purposes.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this resolution seek to eliminate section 390 of the
California Masonic Code and add a new section, 11050, which would authorize
the California Masonic Foundation to provide one or more information and
child ID booths at community and county fairs and the California State Fair, as
it may find appropriate.
All children need protection and many Lodges in this state sponsor
child ID booths with great success. If more lodges were able to participate in
this program, through assistance from the California Masonic Foundation, more
children would be covered in the ID program. The child ID program has proven
to be of great value to local and state authorities as well as giving parents quick
information in time of need.
This program is also a way of letting the public know about one of the
many things Masons do to benefit the public.
This Resolution is consistent with the policies and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

209

Report of the Committee on Finance:
Presently the California Masonic Code requires Grand Lodge to have
at least two booths at the California State Fair. For the past ten years the
California Masonic Foundation has paid for the booth, although it is not
required to do so. If the Foundation were to discontinue support, and Grand
Lodge had to assume paying for the two booths, the cost is estimated to be
$6,000. That would equate to approximately $0.12 of the present per capita.
The Finance Committee has noted that if this legislation passes it
would eliminate a liability for Grand Lodge in the amount of $0.12 in the per
capita, while if it does not pass, this liability would remain. For several years
the California Masonic Foundation has relieved Grand Lodge of this liability by
paying the costs for the two booths at the state fair. However, there is no
guarantee that the Foundation would do so in the future without legislation that
would enable it to provide support to other local fairs throughout the state
instead of just the state fair. There is thus a definite financial liability for Grand
Lodge if the present law remains unchanged.

Resolution ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 06-05
Forfeiture of Fees
The proponents of this Resolution seek to reinstate the rule for forfeiture of fees
and contributions for failure to advance.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
BE IT RESOLVED that Section 26330 of the California Masonic
Code be amended as marked:

§26330.
RESTORATION OF PREVIOUSLY
FORFEITURE OF FEES AND CONTRIBUTIONS.

FORFEITED

FEES

Any Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft who failed to present himself for
advancement to the next higher degree, and whose fees and contributions shall have
been forfeited for failure to advance in accordance with the laws of this Jurisdiction
previously in effect, shall have all such fees and contributions restored as if no
action to forfeit these fees had ever taken place. He shall be restored automatically
as an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft, as the case may be, as if the forfeiture had
never taken place, and shall be entitled to advancement by presenting himself to the
Lodge and complying with the provisions of Section 26340 of this Code.

210

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

When a candidate does not present himself qualified for the Fellow Craft and
Master Mason Degrees within a period of three years from the date of his
initiation as an Entered Apprentice, the Master shall appoint a committee of two
or more members of the Lodge to investigate and report back to the Master the
reasons why the candidate has not presented himself for advancement. If the
candidate does not supply any explanation satisfactory to the Lodge, the Master
shall direct the Secretary to forward to such candidate either by registered letter
or certified mail, with return receipt requested, deposited in the U.S. Post Office,
addressed to him at his last known address, a notice advising him that unless he
shall present himself qualified for advancement, on or before a specified date (at
least 60 days in advance), his fees for the unconferred degrees and all of his
previous contributions shall be declared forfeited.
On the date specified, the Master shall declare the fees and contributions
forfeited unless further time shall have been granted by a vote of the Lodge.
A candidate thus denied advancement shall be considered only as an Entered
Apprentice or Fellow Craft of the Lodge with the right to apply for the
remaining degree or degrees, and again paying the fees and contributions
therefor, subject to investigation and election, the same as any other candidate.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Under current provisions of the California Masonic Code, no time limit
is set for an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft to present himself for
advancement to a higher degree. The lodge in which this non-advancing
candidate originally sought his degrees is required to maintain and account for
an indefinite period, all fees such candidate has paid. In years past, the
California Masonic Code had provided for the forfeiture of fees for a
candidate’s failure to advance. However, at the Annual Communication in
1997, Section 26330 was amended, restoring all such fees as if no forfeiture had
occurred. Accordingly, lodges have been required to carry these non-advancing
candidates on the lodge books for many years. This Resolution would amend
California Masonic Code section 26330 so that if the Entered Apprentice or
Fellow Craft failed to present himself for advancement for a period of three
years, his fees would be forfeited. If the Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft
seeks to advance after his fees have been forfeited, he would have to pay them
again.
While it has long been the policy of this Grand Lodge to encourage the
orderly advancement of candidates, it is also our policy not to burden a lodge
unduly with unnecessary administrative work. If a candidate fails to present
himself for advancement after three years, it may be safely assumed he has no
desire to continue further. Allowing the forfeiture of his fees after this period of
time would ease the administrative burden on the lodge by permitting the
candidate to be dropped from the lodge rolls. Nothing, other than the repayment

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

211

of his degree fees, would prohibit a candidate from seeking advancement so
long as he complies with the provisions of section 26340.
This Resolution is consistent with the policies and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The Finance Committee has determined that there is no financial
impact on Grand Lodge caused by the possible adoption of this measure. While
it might be possible to speculate that more Entered Apprentices and Fellow
Crafts would advance if the possibility that their fees would be forfeited, there is
no evidence that such would be the case, and thus there would probably be no
financial impact on Grand Lodge.

Resolution CARRIED-OVER.
Resolution No. 06-06
Modification to Cipher
The proponents of this Resolution seek to have the lectures in the cipher written
with a bare minimum of key words in abbreviated form or in code, similar to the
booklets authorized by the Grand Lodge of Arizona on May 17, 1994.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
BE IT RESOLVED that Section 26300 of the California Masonic
Code be amended as marked:

§26300. CIPHER RITUALS.
Master Masons are authorized to possess and use an approved cipher ritual.
Grand Lodge shall cause to be prepared, published and sold to Master Masons
only, a cipher ritual of the degrees and lectures, as recognized by Grand Lodge.
Grand Lodge shall also cause to be prepared, published and sold to Master
Masons only, the lectures (including the Middle Chamber Lecture) in a separate
booklet with a bare minimum of key words in abbreviated form or in cipher.
A Master Mason who is an authorized Candidate Coach may reproduce that
portion of the cipher ritual that pertains to the Entered Apprentice and Fellow
Craft proficiency examinations for subsequent utilization by the candidate
during his proficiency.

212

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In no event, however, shall any cipher ritual, or portion thereof, be used during
a tiled meeting except where such meeting is called for the purpose of practicing
the ritual. Such use, however, shall be permitted only by members who are not
participating in the presentation of the ritual.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution 2006-06 seek to amend section 26300 of
the California Masonic Code, Cipher Rituals, to cause the Grand Lodge to form
a committee to design additional separate booklets with the lectures of the
degrees, including the middle chamber lecture, in abbreviated form or code,
similar to booklets published by the Grand Lodge of Arizona on May 17, 1994.
It is unnecessary to form a new committee on ritual, the Grand Lodge
has had a ritual committee for longer than anyone here can remember. It has
been the policy of our Grand Lodge to charge our Ritual Committee with the
responsibility as guardians of our California Ritual, and this Resolution strays
from that policy by creating “a committee designed by the Grand Master” for
the purposes of proposing changes or additions to our existing cipher ritual.
The proponents of this resolution have not justified a need for the
publication of a booklet similar to those published by the Grand Lodge of
Arizona on May 17, 1994.
An examination of all cipher publications in all states will demonstrate
a wide assortment of publications, one not necessarily better than the other, just
different.
It was less than 10 years ago that our Ritual Committee proposed
revisions to our Cipher Rituals, which were adopted by our Grand Lodge. If
there are deficiencies in the current Cipher Rituals, those should either be
identified by our Ritual Committee, or brought to their attention for a proposed
action.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policies and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The cost of the initial production of these booklets is estimated to be
$15,000. Some of the printing costs would be recovered through the sale of the
booklets, but initial production costs would be incurred by Grand Lodge. These
initial production costs are primarliy an estimate of the meetings by the Ritual
Committee to prepare the text of the booklet from the present cipher ritual in the
amount of $12,000. The design and printing of the booklets is estimated to be

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

213

$3,000. The net cost is therefore estimated to be $12,000, or a one-time
increase in the per capita of $0.20.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 06-07
Transfer Life Memberships to Grand Master’s Lodge
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow transfer of life memberships to
the Grand Master’s Lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Recommendation No. 1
creating a “Grand Master’s Lodge” was adopted at the 156th Annual
Communication in October, 2005; and
WHEREAS, Grand Master Kirkpatrick had been asked to bring forth
supplemental legislation which would allow the transfer of a life membership to
the Grand Master’s Lodge if a member eligible for membership in the Grand
Master’s Lodge has such a life membership in the lodge whose charter is
surrendered or revoked; and
WHEREAS, it is desirable that a member be permitted to transfer his
life membership to the Grand Master’s Lodge.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 20085 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§20085. GRAND MASTER’S LODGE.
A special classification of constituent Lodge is hereby established, to be known
as the Grand Master’s Lodge. The Grand Master’s Lodge shall be subject to all
provisions of this Code applicable to constituent Lodges, except as follows:
A.

There shall be only one Grand Master’s Lodge in this Jurisdiction, and
it shall be chartered with the name “Grand Master’s Lodge” without a
Lodge number;

B.

The officers of the Grand Master’s Lodge shall be the Grand Master as
Master, the Senior Grand Warden as Senior Warden, the Junior Grand
Warden as Junior Warden, the Grand Treasurer as Treasurer, and the
Grand Secretary as Secretary, each of whom shall serve in this office
ex officio;

C.

Membership shall consist only of:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

214

1.

As ex-officio members, the Grand Master, the Deputy Grand
Master, the Senior Grand Warden, the Junior Grand Warden,
the Grand Treasurer and the Grand Secretary; and

2.

Those Entered Apprentices, Fellow Crafts and Master Masons
of a constituent Lodge at the time that the Lodge’s charter is
surrendered or revoked who are not at the time a plural or dual
member in another Lodge;

D.

Membership in the Grand Master’s Lodge shall cease for any member
upon taking a dimit therefrom or by a plural or dual membership in
another Lodge;

E.

The Grand Master’s Lodge shall hold no meetings nor confer any
degrees but may request courtesy work for its Entered Apprentices and
Fellow Crafts in another Lodge;

F.

The provisions of this Code requiring certain recommendations,
notifications and certificates from an Inspector dealing with
qualifications of officers and membership in a Lodge seeking a
Dispensation or Charter shall not apply to the Grand Master’s Lodge.
Its Dispensation and Charter shall be issued without charge or vote of
Grand Lodge;

G.

The forms of Petition for Dispensation, Petition for Charter, Charter
and Uniform Code of By-Laws shall be adopted insofar as the
provisions may be applicable to the Grand Master’s Lodge, except as
required to comply with the provisions of this Section;

H.

The provisions of this Code dealing with the qualifications of the
Master, Senior Warden and Junior Warden of a constituent Lodge shall
not apply to the officers of the Grand Master’s Lodge;

I.

The Grand Master’s Lodge shall not be entitled to representation in
Grand Lodge or any vote therein;

J.

Dues for the operation of the Grand Master’s Lodge shall be
established by the Grand Master each year in an amount sufficient to
offset any cost to Grand Lodge for the operation of the Grand Master’s
Lodge, imposing no burden on the per capita. These dues shall be
sufficient not only to pay the costs of the operation of the Grand
Master’s Lodge, but also the per capita due Grand Lodge each year;
and

K.

The life members of a constituent Lodge at the time that the Lodge’s
charter is surrendered or revoked who become members of the Grand
Master’s Lodge shall be life members of the Grand Master’s Lodge and
shall be forever exempt from the payment of dues in the Grand
Master’s Lodge. Any other provision of this Code to the contrary

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

215

notwithstanding, no such life member shall be entitled to payment of
his interest in the Life Membership Fund of the dissolved Lodge. His
interest in the Life Membership Fund of the dissolved Lodge shall be
kept by Grand Lodge in a separate fund, and the interest or dividends
paid in cash on such fund shall be used to offset any cost to Grand
Lodge for the operation of the Grand Master’s Lodge. If his
membership in the Grand Master’s Lodge shall cease for any reason,
his life membership in the Grand Master’s Lodge shall cease also; and
L.K.

All other actions requiring the consent or vote of the Lodge shall be
effected by the Grand Master acting alone.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
This Resolution would permit the transfer to the Grand Master’s Lodge
those life membership funds held by a discontinued or charter-revoked lodge
whose life members become members of the Grand Master’s Lodge. Under this
Resolution, those members of the Grand Master’s Lodge whose life
memberships have been so transferred would be “forever exempt from payment
of dues in the Grand Master’s Lodge.”
The Grand Master’s Lodge is a “virtual lodge” which holds no
meetings, confers no degrees and has no involvement in legislation of the Grand
Lodge or of any given lodge. The Grand Master’s Lodge was designed to be a
“holding process” to enable brethren to have the benefits of the Masonic
fraternity until they can affiliate with another lodge.
Under the present Code, all of the monetary assets, including life
membership funds, of a lodge that has lost its charter are transferred to Grand
Lodge. This Resolution would change that process by diverting these life
membership funds to the Grand Master’s Lodge, thereby effectively funding a
“virtual lodge.”
This Resolution does not address the issue of investment of the
transferred life membership funds. No investment committee was created when
the Grand Master’s Lodge was established based on Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s
Recommendation Number One at the 156th Annual Communication.
Furthermore, while this Resolution provides that the new life members would be
“forever exempt from payment of dues in the Grand Master’s Lodge.” This
Resolution does not address whether annual per capita would have to be paid for
the new life members of the Grand Master’s Lodge. These issues should be
addressed before this Resolution is enacted.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policies and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

216

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The section of the code establishing the Grand Master’s Lodge requires
the dues to be set in an amount to cover the costs of operating the lodge. Since
the dues would take into consideration the income received from the life
membership funds – if any – there would be no resultant cost to Grand Lodge if
this legislation were adopted.

Resolution ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 06-08
Masonic Relief for Masonic Youth Group Members
The proponents of this Resolution seek to add members in good standing of the
Masonic Youth Orders to the list of persons authorized to receive Masonic
relief.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, recent flooding in the Vacaville area last winter caused
two of our Rainbow Girls to lose most of their possessions; and
WHEREAS, our Charity Committee was unable to provide emergency
relief to these girls due to the limitations imposed by the California Masonic
Code.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 1 of Article
X of the Uniform Lodge Bylaws set forth in Section 19040 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:
The Master and Wardens shall be a Charity Committee, and shall have the
power to draw upon the Treasurer for any sum, not exceeding $_______ at any
one time for the relief of a distressed worthy Brother, his wife, widow, or
orphan, and a member in good standing (other than a senior or majority
member) of any of the Masonic Youth Orders.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that Section 28010 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§28010. DUTIES OF LODGE
A Lodge must relieve its own members, their widows and orphans, and a
member in good standing (other than a senior or majority member) of any of the
Masonic Youth Orders, when in distress, regardless of their place of residence,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

217

according to its own circumstances and ability. It shall see that its needy
members do not suffer or want, or become a burden to others.
An "orphan" is the son or daughter under the age of majority of a deceased
Master Mason. No man or woman over the age of majority shall be entitled to
relief as a matter of right. However, a Lodge may, in its discretion, provide
relief in exceptional circumstances to the adult child of a deceased Master
Mason where a need arises by reason of a physical or mental impairment.
A "widow" is one who was married to a Master Mason at the time of his demise.
A widow who remarries forfeits all rights to Masonic relief from her previous
husband pursuant to this section even though such remarriage is later terminated
by divorce, dissolution, or otherwise.
Money given a Brother as a charity by a Lodge cannot be charged to him.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to add active members of the
Masonic Youth Orders to the list of persons authorized to receive Masonic relief
under the provisions of California Masonic Code section 28010, the section
which specifies the duties of a lodge. While it may have true Masonic appeal in
it its conception, this Resolution is overbroad in its application. The Resolution
does not specify which lodge is to be responsible for the members of the youth
orders who are in need. There are lodges which do not sponsor youth orders,
but this resolution appears to make all lodges in this state responsible for all
members of youth orders in this state. While this legislation is clearly well
intended, it seems to be legislating for the exception, rather than the rule. The
specific reference, by the proponents, to the flooding in Vacaville, is a case in
point.
There is further ambiguity under this resolution. As an example, it is
unclear whether a member of a youth order could apply for relief from one
lodge, while his or her family applies for relief from another lodge. Nor is it
clear to which lodge a member of a youth order should apply for relief, in the
event he or she lives in an area which has no lodge or Masonic Service Bureau.
Any member of a youth order who suffers a loss from a disaster and is
in need, may apply directly to the Grand Master for relief through the Grand
Master’s disaster relief fund.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policies and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

218

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The Finance Committee has determined that there is no financial
impact on Grand Lodge caused by the possible adoption of this measure,
because it would be a local lodge option.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.
Resolution No. 06-09
Formulation of Annual Dues of Constituent Lodges
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow for rounding up of Lodge dues
based in part on the annual Grand Lodge per capita.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the current California Masonic Code allows for the
constituent Lodges to specify in their By-Laws that their annual dues be either a
fixed amount or a fixed amount plus the Per Capita sum approved by Grand
Lodge at the immediately preceding Annual Communication; and
WHEREAS, using the latter form is attractive because it conveniently
relieves each Lodge from the need to increase its dues whenever the Per Capita
sum is increased by Grand Lodge; and
WHEREAS, using the latter form would still require each Lodge to
annually adjust the dues of each member and could potentially result in such a
strange and unfriendly total value as $63.57; and
WHEREAS, being able to round the total to some more reasonable
value and have it remain at that value for a reasonable period, would appear
more prudent to the brethren and would be advantageous for the Secretaries.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 1 of Article
IX of the Uniform Lodge Bylaws set forth in Section 19040 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

Section 1. The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be $_____ per annum,
payable in advance on the first day of January.
Or in the alternative
The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be the sum of $______ plus the
Per Capita sum approved by Grand Lodge at the immediately preceding Annual
Communication, per annum, payable in advance on the first day of January.
Or in the alternative

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

219

The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be the sum of $ ______ plus the
Per Capita sum approved by Grand Lodge at the immediately preceding Annual
Communication, with the total thus determined rounded up to the next higher
whole multiple of $ ______, per annum, payable in advance on the first day of
January.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow for the “rounding up”
of lodge dues when those dues result in a figure that would be less than a whole
dollar amount, as distinguished from what the proponents call a “strange and
unfriendly total value [such] as $63.57.” Presently, a lodge is permitted to
charge a fixed, whole dollar sum, for dues, a sum which includes the Grand
Lodge per capita charge. In the alternative, a lodge may require its members to
pay a base amount for dues, then add to it the Grand Lodge per capita. It is this
latter formula which may result in a “strange and unfriendly total.”
The rounding up of dues, by a matter of cents, would be of benefit to a
lodge, making its accounting easier, without creating any serious financial
hardship to lodge members who pay their dues on a yearly basis. Thus, so long
as the lodge has provided for the payment of its full per capita assessment, this
Resolution makes sense, spelled “s-e-n-s-e.”
This Resolution is consistent with the policies and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The Finance Committee has determined that there is no financial
impact on Grand Lodge caused by the possible adoption of this measure,
because it would be a local lodge option.

Resolution CARRIED-OVER.

Acacia Creek Committee
By Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
In 2000, the Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California
embarked on a comprehensive and long-range strategic planning process for the
organization arising out of our commitment to our historical mission and the
need to develop a long-term, sustainable financial strategy. This was undertaken
out of the recognition that the Homes needs to increase sources of revenue in
addition to investment earnings on its endowment and general funds and address
the challenges of health care inflationary rates which are 2.5 times greater than
standard inflation. Without identifying new sources of revenue by 2009, the
Homes projected difficulty in being able to maintain current levels of service,
including the substantial growth experienced in our Masonic Outreach Services
program.
A part of this strategic planning process included:
¾

A written survey in 1999 completed by 2438 fraternal members

¾

Focus groups in 2001 attended by 184 members

¾

Meetings with Trustees and Executives from three other Masonic
Homes (Ohio, Pennsylvania and Connecticut) to discuss their
growth strategies

¾

12 informational sessions conducted in 2005 and attended by 448
members

¾

Membership demographic analysis and projections

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA
¾

225

Research into state and federal industry trends

From this research, the Board learned that many members of our aging
fraternity are struggling with the lack of affordable options for residential
services in California. We also learned that – all other things being equal – 98%
would prefer Masonic-sponsored services.

THE VISION FOR THE ACACIA CREEK COMMUNITIES
After four years of research, the Board of Trustees arrived at a concept
for creating two new Masonic senior living communities on our existing
campuses. Both campuses have a significant amount of land that currently lies
fallow. Creating new retirement communities on this land will create a revenue
producing asset for the Masonic Homes as well as a new source of service and
security for our fraternal family.
These new communities are both planned as Continuing Care
Retirement Communities (CCRCs) which means that they provide an active
vibrant community for independent seniors as well as more advanced levels of
care - should the need arise.
The new communities have been named Acacia Creek at Covina and
Acacia Creek at Union City and admissions to these communities are offered to
California Master Masons, their wives, widows and mothers.
Acacia Creek at Covina will include 171 new residences and Acacia
Creek at Union City will include 189 new residences. On both campuses,
residents will be offered a variety of residence options– ranging from two
bedroom units of 865 sq feet to three bedroom cottages of1650 square feet. The
communities will include new dining facilities, indoor pools and recreation
programs and separate Assisted Living and Memory Support care.
Masonic Homes of California will always be open to all eligible
Masons – retardless of their financial assets. Residents may enter our existing
facilities if they have $10 or $10,000,000 and be treated the same once they are
there.
Because we cannot afford any additional claims on our charitable
dollars, Acacia Creek residents will have to financially qualify for admission.
Residents will pay a refundable entrance fee and an ongoing monthly fee.
Founders – those who sign up before construction - are offered a 100%
refundable entrance fee, which means that when they leave the community, all
of their entrance fee will be returned to them or their heirs. After construction,
residents will pay a 90% refundable entrance fee, which means that when they
leave the community, 90% of their entrance fee will be returned to them or their
heirs.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

226

ORGANIZATIONAL STRUCTURE
The mission of the Acacia Creek (AC) corporations is to serve
members of our fraternal family and support the mission of the Masonic Homes
of California.
Two separate corporations were created and have applied for tax
exempt (501(c)3) status. Masonic Homes of California is the sole corporate
member of each Acacia entity and elects the Acacia Creek boards, subject to
approval by the Grand Master. These separate corporations insulate the
Masonic Homes by:
¾

Protecting and isolating the Endowment and General funds from
operations, liabilities and risks of Acacia Creeks

¾

Protecting and preserving the current admission policies of
existing facilities

The initial Board of Trustees for each of the Acacia Creek corporations
is identical and includes seven members:
a representative of the Executive Committee of Grand Lodge:
Larry Adamson, President of the Board,
three overlapping members of the Masonic Homes Board:
Kenneth Nagel
Michael Neben, Vice President of the Board
Philip McAllister,
and two trustees from outside the MHC Board of Trustees:
Frank Loui (lodge #)
Ara Maloyan (lodge #)

THE DEVELOPMENT PROCESS
The process for development of the Acacia Creek projects has been
designed in a manner that allows us to minimize our investment while we test
the membership’s interest and demand for such projects. As one Masonic
Homes Trustee aptly put it, “Our approach is not ‘if we build it they will come –
rather we will build it IF they come.” The development process is structured in
phases. Depending on the success of each phase, the Board then votes to move
forward or abandon the projects.
Phase One – the Preliminary Feasibility Stage – was just completed.
During this phase, we:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

227

¾

Conducted focus groups

¾

Completed a demographic review and exhaustive competitive
analysis on retirement services already available in California

¾

Completed conceptual master-planning and design work

¾

Developed proposed program guide, pricing plan, marketing plan
and budget

¾

Completed initial financial feasibility studies

¾

Met with City Officials and submitted land use applications

The results of this phase demonstrated significant interest on the part of
the membership and that the available land offered us the opportunity to
affordably build new communities on our existing campuses. With this, the
Trustees voted to enter into the next phase of development.
Phase Two – The Marketing Excitement Period – just began and is
expected to run through the end of the 2006. During this phase, we accept
$1000 priority reservation fees from those interested in admission. We have set
a goal of receiving 200 priority deposits for each campus. If the priority sales
goals are not met, project spending stops or goes forward only at the successful
site. All $1000 deposits are fully refunded to our fraternal constituents.
The membership is expressing great interest and excitement in these
projects; at the time of writing this report, approximately two and a half months
into the excitement period, we have reached 50% of our goal for the entire
excitement phase. If the present pace of sales continues, it appears likely we will
enter into the next phase of development.
Phase Three – Pre-sales and architectural drawings – is anticipated to
last 12-14 months. Four key milestones must be met during Phase Three:
¾

Receiving state review and approval of project feasibility

¾

70% presales per site (20% deposits)

¾

Architectural drawings completed and City permits secured

¾

Construction costs bid and finalized

¾

Financing commitments secured by Acacia Creek corporations
when presales goals have been completed.

Again, should we fail to meet our presales goals, project spending stops
or goes forward only at the successful site. All monies are fully refunded to
fraternal constituents.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

228

Phase Four – Construction and Move In
Construction is anticipated to take 20 months – so should we move
forward – residents will start moving in 2009.
The Board of Trustees and Executive Staff of the Masonic Homes, as
well as the Trustees of the newly formed Acacia Creek boards, are excited by
the potential of these new communities. Four years of research has led us to this
point and we believe that these communities make sense for fraternity and for
the Masonic Homes. These communities will generate revenue to support the
charitable mission of the Masonic Home and serve more of our members in
need.
Through the new Acacia Creek corporations, we will be able to serve
an additional 580 elderly members of our fraternal family.
In addition, the Acacia Creek projects will generate revenue for the
Masonic Homes of California through land leases and management fees. The
revenue generated would allow the Masonic Homes to care for an additional (on
an annual basis):
¾

87 seniors in residential services at the Masonic Homes OR

¾

99 children in homes from neglect and abuse

¾

309 seniors given the choice to remain in their own home and
home community, while being cared for by our Masonic Outreach
Services (MOS) department.

Given that these projects allow us to put unused land to work for us –
and work for our fraternity – and that they fill an unmet need among our
constituents, while generating revenue to support the charitable mission of the
Masonic Homes, we are excited by our prospects. Should the membership
continue to respond so favorably, we look forward to 2009 when we stand
before two new communities built by and for our Masonic brethren.
Admission Committee
By Gary Yanes, Chairman
The Admission Review Program of the Board is a carefully developed
analysis and evaluation process that continues to be improved and refined to
meet the evolving needs of our Applicants, as well as the Homes Staff and the
Board.
The interview process and on site evaluation is done at the same time
the applicant visits one of the two facilities. A Staff report on this overnight
visit, together with the Medical Director’s comments on his/her review of the
applicant’s medical history and data, is then added to the application

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

229

information. The staff then develops an executive summary on the applicants
and presents that to the Admission Committee for review and approval.
Anytime the Staff recommends against admission the full application is given to
the Admissions Committee for detailed review and discussion during the
Admissions Committee meeting. Any application that is denied requires the
confirmation of the full Board.
Prior to the regular Admission Committee meeting, held on the second
Tuesday of each month, the six Trustees, who form the Admission Committee,
receive and carefully study the admission summary for each prospective resident
or client of the Masonic Homes of California. The Trustees review and evaluate
the applications and accompanying data, and discuss their viewpoint during the
Admissions Committee meeting.
The Home’s Executive Staff attend
Admissions Committee meetings to answer questions and provide additional
information to members of the committee. The recommendations and comments
of the committee are presented to the full Board for confirmation.
The number of applications considered by the Admission Committee
for couples, individuals and children continues to vary from month to month.
From July 1, 2005 to June 30, 2006, 47 residents were admitted (38 on partial
assignment of assets and 9 on fee for Service). Union City currently serves 267
residents (66 on full assignment of assets, 182 on partial assignment of assets,
and 19 on fee for service); Covina currently serves 74 senior residents (14 on
full assignment of assets, 58 on partial assignment of assets and 2 on monthly
fee). For this time period, there were 75 applications completed by our brothers,
widows, and mothers.
The Masonic Outreach Services program (formerly known as NonResident Assistance program) continues to grow and is an effective program in
assisting our eligible Brethren and their families or widows. This program
provides the necessary support, financial, housing and/or care management
assistance to applicants who are in need of this type of assistance to remain in
their present community or residence. The Masonic Outreach Service program
currently serves 103 clients. This year the committee reviewed and approved 28
applications for this program. Members of the staff monitor these individuals on
a regular basis to ensure that their needs are met.
During fiscal year 2004-2005 the average cost per resident day by level
of care was as follows: Independent Living $169 per day, Assisted Living
$238per day, Skilled Nursing Care $333 per day, Dementia $284and MOS
$78per day.
The Covina facility provides hospice care for its terminally ill
residents, thus giving its residents a palliative care option previously only
available through the placement in a skilled nursing setting. The Union City
facility provides specialty care to residents with memory impairment in a
customized dementia care unit on campus.

230

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In 2006, the Children’s Program at Covina served an average monthly
census of 30 children; and was accredited by the California Alliance of Children
and Family Services. The Admissions Committee also oversees admissions to
the children’s program. In 2006, 15 admissions were made to the program.
Eleven children were reunified with their families or transferred to more
appropriate services.
Our admission criteria provide that children and grandchildren of
Master Masons in good standing in need of care or who are abandoned, abused,
and neglected are given first priority. If space is available admission
consideration is given to non- Masonic children, who are in need of care or who
are abandoned, abused, and neglected; and who have a high potential for self
sufficiency, academic success, post graduate readiness, emotional and social
well-being, family reunification, and good health.
I sincerely thank the other Trustee members of the Admission
Committee who labor long and hard over the applications. They are Michael
Day, Albert Gates, Richard Hopper, Phillip McAllister, Ken Nagel, and Mike
Neben. I also extend my appreciation to the Executive Staff of Masonic Homes
and their staff.

Children’s Program Committee
By Mark Gibson, Chairman
Consistent with our Masonic principles, the Board of Trustees and staff
of the Masonic Homes’ Children’s Program constantly seek improvement in all
that we do, evidenced by the recently adopted “Circle of Courage” philosophy.
Since 2004 the “Circle of Courage” philosophy of care promotes belonging and
healthy relationships, teaches the mastery of skills, developing self-sufficiency
and independence, and encourages the virtue of generosity so that children can
become successful, contributing members of society. At the same time the
Board adopted this new philosophy of care, they also revised the admissions
criteria and developed outcome measurements to track the success of this new
model of care.
In June 2006, Marissa Espinoza, Director of Children’s Services, gave
a presentation to the Board on the success of this new model of care. The
results were impressive.
In addition to ongoing therapeutic services which averaged
approximately 92 individual, parent, family, and group sessions per month
during FY 2005-06, the Children’s Program also maintained a 6:1 children to
tutor ratio to maximize children’s academic successes. The program proudly
supported an average of 12 children in Honors/College prep courses during the
last academic year. The story of our children’s grade point averages (GPA) said
it all:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

231

•

Children in the program less than six months had an average GPA
of 1.46.

•

Children in the program six months to one year had an average
GPA of 2.47.

•

Children in the program over one year had an average GPA of
3.00.

Another shift is children’s length of stay within the program. Instead
of long-term placement under which the program historically has operated, the
focus has become family reunification whenever possible. The staff of the
Masonic Homes’ Children’s Program actively engaged the whole family in
therapy as indicated to meet this goal. We ensure the parents and guardians
participate in parenting program classes and family open houses and we monitor
and provide assistance to families in overcoming obstacles for reunification.
The program includes helping parents identify new housing in safer
neighborhoods, securing employment and identifying appropriate transportation.
Since 2004, the Children’s Program has successfully and safely reunited 25
children with their families.
To ensure their long-term success, the Children’s Program also served
71 families through our Family Resource Services, which provides counseling
and other supportive services. We also assisted an average of 12 families per
month in accessing housing, food, furniture, public social services, job
placement and outside community mental health services. Through Family
Resource Services, we support families prior to admission as well as postreunification to ensure that the family is stable once the child is reunified. The
lives that are transformed by our services are the most important story. To
highlight the success of just four of our recent graduates:
•

Carl began his fourth year at CSU-Fresno where he is majoring in
Psychology.
Carl was awarded $13,380 in Masonic Homes
Scholarship support for the 2006-07 school year.

•

Lauren will be entering her first year at Azusa Pacific University and
intends to major in Psychology and Deaf Studies. Lauren was awarded
$20,828 in Masonic Homes Scholarship support for the 2006-07 school
year.

•

Austin will be entering Citrus College and majoring in Graphic Design.
Austin was awarded $2,230 in Masonic Homes Scholarship support
for the 2006-07 school year.

•

Cynthia will be attending Long Beach City College to complete her
general education as she pursues a degree in Veterinarian Medical

Studies. Cynthia was awarded $4,880 in Masonic Homes Scholarship
support for the 2006-07 school year.

Thanks to the commitment of the Masons of California, the members

a time.
