Finance Committee
By Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
The role of the Finance Committee is to review and recommend
approval to the Board of Trustees the operating and capital budgets. The
Committee also monitors actual performance against those approved budgets.
The Committee also reviews recommendations from the Investment Committee
of Grand Lodge regarding investment strategies for Home’s funds as well as
recommendation for spending guidelines that establish parameters for operating
and capital expenditures. The Committee works with recommendations from
the Insurance Committee of Grand Lodge to establish financially sound risk
management and coverage structures.
The Finance Committee reviews and approves the reports of the
external auditors as well as special internal audits that are conducted from time
to time. From these audits, the Committee works with management to develop
appropriate action plans. In collaboration with the Strategic Planning
Committee, the Finance Committee oversees long term financial planning as
well as the review of financial analysis relating to new programs, operational
initiatives and capital projects. The Committee advises the Board as to the
appropriate sources of funding for all proposed projects and programs.
The Masonic Homes of California revenue ended fiscal year 2006 with
a favorable budget variance of just over _%, resulting from ___ than expected
resident related revenue. Expenses also ended the year favorable to budget just
under _%. This performance required careful management of expenses in light
of ever increasing costs associated with employee benefits, insurance coverage
and increased demand for Masonic Outreach Services (MOS). We are pleased
to report that overall expenses for the Masonic Homes have continued to remain
relatively flat for the past 5 years, despite the fact that the rate of inflation in
health care related expenses has exceeded the general rate of inflation by two
and half times.
For the past several years, operating expenditures of the Homes have
been guided by spending parameters set as a percentage of a five-year rolling
average of the investment balances of the general and endowment funds. This
approach insures operating expense will be somewhat buffered from significant
year-to-year market swings while protecting the integrity of the fund balances
for future generations of residents. The ____ performance of the financial
markets during the past year has helped to mitigate some of the expected decline
in this spending calculation. However, continuing high inflation in the health
care industry is putting a strain on our ability to operate the Homes within this
guideline. The Committee, Board and Staff continue to evaluate the operations
of the Homes to increase efficiencies, without sacrificing our high level of care
while maintaining the fiscal health and strength of the Homes. In addition, the
Committee, Board and Staff continue to explore opportunities to diversify the

234

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

revenue sources of the Homes to better protect the operation for future
generations.
The Finance Committee reviewed regular reports from the Grand
Treasurer. These reports include quarterly investment performance reports,
monthly operating statements, quarterly capital project reviews and various
others. Moss Adams LLP, the new auditor for Grand Lodge and the Masonic
Homes, completed the annual external audit for the fiscal year ended June 30,
2005. Moss Adams gave the Homes a “clean” audit by issuing an unqualified
opinion on the completeness and accuracy of the financial reports.
In 2006, the Director of Finance of the Grand Lodge of California,
Mark Koenig, left the organization to pursue another opportunity with a nonprofit organization in San Francisco. We had been delighted with Mr. Koenig’s
service as key staff to our Committee. However, the Chairman of the Finance
Committee was a key participant in the Committee that recruited Frank Tsai as
the new Chief Financial Officer of the Grand Lodge. Mr. Tsai brings many
years of experience as a CFO in senior living communities as well as other nonprofit organizations. He began at the end of FY 2006.
The Finance Committee worked with management to develop the fiscal
year 2007 budget, based on the direction that management use a “zero-based”
approach in formulating their budget recommendations. In the face of ever
rising costs, management proposed, the Finance Committee recommended, and
the Board approved a budget which once again came in below the 5% spending
guideline.
The Finance Committee also worked with management to develop a
capital project budget to assure the Homes remain a well maintained facility and
protect the comfort and safety of our residents. The Homes continues to fully
fund the Capital Reserve fund from annual depreciation through the operating
budget. This fund is used to pay for capital projects and to build a reserve for
future needs. The Committee along with management developed a fiscal year
2006 capital budget which will allow the balance in this fund to grow for future
large capital needs.
Securing earthquake coverage at affordable rates for our facilities, both
of which are located in earthquake zones, proved a particular challenge in 2006.
As a part of the FY 2007 budget, at the recommendation of the Finance
Committee, the Board approved additional funding of our Homes Self-Insured
Risk fund, to compensate for the fact that our levels of earthquake insurance
were reduced.
Along with the Strategic Planning Committee, Board and Staff, the
Finance Committee is reviewing and monitoring several possible major strategic
initiatives. These initiatives include continuation of a phased in development of
additional independent living units, known as Acacia Creek, Masonic Living
Communities, at both the Union City and Covina campuses. If these

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

235

communities are eventually built, all money loaned to the new Acacia Creek
corporations out of non-endowment related funding from the Masonic Homes,
will be repaid to the Homes. These additional units will allow the Homes to
serve more members of the fraternity and to reach out to Masons previously
underserved by our current facilities. In addition, these developments will allow
the Homes to more effectively and efficiently use the current infrastructure of
the Homes.
Special projects reviewed by the Committee included the addition of an
elevator to the Adams building at the Union City campus. At the time this
report is being written, this project is still in progress.
The Committee and Staff, working with the Grand Secretary of the
Grand Lodge, completed an extensive review of our employee benefit plans,
including cost structures. In addition to the conversion to a Paid Time Off
system implemented in the prior year to replace sick leave and vacation benefits,
we increased employee participation in the premium costs of their medical and
dental insurance. This change acknowledged that the cost of employee health
benefits is one of our most rapidly rising costs and it is increasingly a standard
among employers to have employees participate in these costs. The Committee
continues to close review and monitor other employee benefit programs to
mitigate the increasing costs of these programs.
In 2005 the Board approved the creation of a separate Audit Committee
for the Homes in order to promote transparency and openness of the financial
operations of the Homes, and to comply with State regulations, through Grand
Lodge, This Committee will work with the external, independent auditors to
ensure the financial reports of the Homes is accurate, timely and complies with
all necessary requirements. The Audit Committee serves as the Audit
Committee for the Grand Lodge and all Masonic Related Entities. This allows
the Committee to gain experience and knowledge of all operations and provide a
more knowledgeable level of over site.
The Committee and Staff continued to monitor the cost structure of the
Homes, promoting continuous improvement in costs without sacrificing the high
quality care affording to our residents. A key factor in the effective
management of costs in 2006 was the implementation of new guidelines to
balance the acuity of residents in the two Homes. Since this change we have
seen an increase in the ratio of independent to assisted living residents. An
increasing proportion of assisted living applicants are being cared for
immediately through our Masonic Outreach Services (MOS) program.
The Committee continues to closely monitor the MOS program. This
program provides services to Masons and their families in their homes and
communities. The program has been extremely successful. In the fiscal year
ended June 30, 2006, the expenses associated with this program increase more
than __% over the prior year. The Committee continues to work with Staff to

236

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

develop and implement strategies to manage the growth in this program within
the constraints of the budget and out financial resources. However, as noted
previously, in spite of this increase in MOS expenses, the overall expenses of
the Homes ended the year under budget due to effective cost management.

Strategic Planning Committee
By George L. Geanoulis
STRATEGIC PLANNING
In November 2003, the Board of Trustees adopted a three-year
strategic plan for the Masonic Homes of California. This strategic plan guided
and directed the efforts of the Trustees and staff since that time. Many of the
initiatives set forth are multi-year projects and still in progress. Given this, this
Committee recently approved an update to the original plan.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

237

In the 2003 plan, three strategic imperatives were set forth that set the
foundation for our activities. These same imperatives frame and direct our
activities today:
¾

Protecting and furthering the mission of the Masonic Homes of
California;

¾

Protecting and optimizing the financial resources of the organization;
and,

¾

Positioning the organization to respond to the changing and emerging
needs of the membership.

THE PROCESS
Our strategic planning process is comprehensive and involves
exhaustive research into the latest thinking in senior and children’s services as
well as social and market trends that are impacting the lives of our members –
today and in the future. Most importantly, we spend a tremendous amount of
time in hearing directly from our membership about their needs and desires. In
the two years leading up to the development of the 2003 strategic plan and in
the subsequent three years, we have conducted multiple focus groups and
surveys and ongoing research to learn about the needs and desires of our
membership. Over the past three years, our research has included (but has not
been limited to):
¾

Four Informational sessions at Annual Communication in 2005; 93
members completed an exit survey

¾

Nine informational sessions in December 2005 across the state; 355
members completed an extensive exit survey

¾

In 2006, we have conducted an average of 24 informational sessions
each month across the state.

¾

Gave presentations at High Twelve Meetings, Stated Meetings,
Secretaries Associations and other events by invitation

¾

Extensive market study of available services and pricing in California;
research into membership and demographic trends; completion of a
thorough market assessment and marketing plan for proposed new
senior living communities.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

238

OUR CHALLENGES
As detailed in the President’s and Executive Vice President Reports
our organization must identify a sustainable, long-term funding strategy for the
organization. Without this, we will not be able to maintain our current level of
service by 2009. To this end, a key component of our strategic plan is to
develop new revenue producing strategies.

FRONTAGE PROPERTY AT UNION CITY
A new strategic opportunity with potentially significant economic and
service benefits to our organization is currently being explored by this
committee. We are in very early discussions with possible partners about
creating a mixed-used, commercial and residential development on the frontage
property in Union City. Such a development would generate significant land
lease revenue to the Masonic Homes and increase the value of our land. As
currently envisioned, such a venture would involve little risk on the part of the
Masonic Homes; we would provide the land and a developer would bear all the
upfront development cost and labor. This development will take place under
long-term leases and under no circumstances, would we consider selling any
land.
This initiative is just getting underway as we identify the challenges
and opportunities of such a venture and conduct feasibility and market studies.

ACUTE REHABILITATION HOSPITAL
Since the creation of the last strategic plan, we are now exploring the
possibility of building a new acute rehabilitation hospital on our Union City
campus. Such a facility offers several distinct advantages:
¾

Fills an unmet need for our resident population and in the community

¾

Allows us to create a new source of revenue to fund our charitable
mission.

We are in the very early stages of this project and will be working on
determining its feasibility in the coming year.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

239

SENIOR HOUSING PROJECTS
The 2003 strategic plan identified the development of new market-rate
retirement communities for our middle-income constituents as a possibility to be
explored. At the time of that plan, it was undecided as to whether such
communities were feasible and where they should be located. For three years,
we have actively pursued these initiatives.

The Acacia Creek Communities
The Board of Trustees has arrived at a concept for creating two new
Masonic senior living communities on the unused land of our existing
campuses. If built, these new communities will serve more of our fraternal
constituents and create a new revenue stream for the Masonic Homes.
Acacia Creek at Covina will include 171 new residences and Acacia
Creek at Union City will include 189 new residences. On both campuses,
residents will be offered a variety of residence options– ranging from two
bedroom units of 865 sq feet to three bedroom cottages of 1650 square feet.
The communities will include new dining facilities, indoor pools and recreation
programs and separate Assisted Living and Memory Support care.
This committee invested a significant amount of time completing
extensive feasibility and market studies. We are gratified to see how favorably
the membership is responding to this concept. This initiative is now being
directed by the Acacia Creek Board of Directors.

Masonic Outreach Services (MOS)
Our MOS program has grown significantly over the past three years –
in fact we’ve seen a growth of over 35% since the time of the 2003 strategic
plan. During the past three years, we’ve restructured our programs and services
to accomplish the following:
¾

Developed a new centralized intake department to streamline and
improve the admission policies for all of our communities

¾

Created procedures for serving those in need of Assisted Living
through community partnerships

¾

Created a new case management program for those needing help
accessing necessary services (without needing financial support)

¾

Accessing all forms of available reimbursement to reduce the cost per
case

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

240
¾

Increased emphasis on lodge and family involvement in the care and
support of our MOS clients

CHILDREN’S SERVICES
In addition to identifying and responding to the strategic initiatives for
our senior programs, this committee also formed a sub-committee to study the
needs of our children and the future of our children’s residential program. As a
result of the efforts of this committee, in 2003 a new program model and
philosophy of care was adopted. The emphasis was shifted from a childcentered program to a family-focused program with a goal of family
reunification whenever possible. The focus was also on developing effective
program outcome measurements as well as developing a Family Resource
Center that would allow us to serve children and family on an outpatient basis.
In 2005, the program adopted the “Circle of Courage” philosophy,
which is a philosophy of care that promotes belonging and healthy relationships,
teaches the mastery of skills, develops self-sufficiency and independence and
encourages the virtue of generosity so that children can be successful,
contributing members of society.
The Children’s Committee continues to focus on the outcomes of the
program and have been gratified by the increase in the grade point averages of
the children and the successful family reunifications that have taken place.
In addition, we are very the Family Resource Center became
operational. In the first year of operations, 63 individuals received some form
of care, which could include: transitional support, case-management resources,
independent living skills, parenting classes, summer day camp, continuing
education training and other forms of support.

CONCLUSION
Preserving and protecting our charitable mission and providing the best
possible care to our residents and clients is the primary focus of the Strategic
Planning Committee of Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California.
We are committed to creating a long-term, sustainable financial plan for the
organization that ensures the continuity and perpetuity of our historical mission.
In closing, the Strategic Planning Committee and its Chairman would
like to acknowledge the leadership and support of the entire Board of Trustees
of the Masonic Homes and its President, Allen B. Gresham, PGM. Together we
are committed to upholding the standards of excellence demanded of us by the
guiding principles of freemasonry and to ensure our services are delivered in a

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

241

manner befitting our founding ideals. It is an honor and a privilege to be a
member of this Board.
President’s Report
By Allen B. Gresham
The Masonic Homes of California were founded over 108 years ago to
provide relief to our brethren in need– seniors and children. Today in
residential communities and outreach programs, we serve the most vulnerable
members of our fraternal family in a manner befitting our founding ideals.
Our founding purpose and our guiding ideals motivate us to ensure that
our services reflect the changing needs of the fraternity and are relevant to the
age in which we live. Most importantly, they obligate us to protect our heritage
and resources for today’s residents and those of tomorrow.
Our research into the needs of today’s members tell us that the
membership is aging with over 50% of us over the age of 70. Our
demographics show us that demand for aging services will remain high for the
next 15-20 years. Further, we know that this senior population primarily falls
into two categories:
¾

The frail and impoverished

¾

The middle-income and active

To best serve our fraternal family, it is our responsibility to create
programs and services that reflect these varied needs. In recognition of this, in
2005, the Board of Trustees created a new vision statement that expanded the
role of the Masonic Homes in providing Relief to our brethren. No longer are
we simply a “place of last resort” but we are committed to “providing an
innovative array of services and programs that support people’s journey through
life’s transitions.”

OUR MISSION IN ACTION
Children’s Services
In November 2004, the Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of
California adopted a new model of care for our Children’s program. The
“Circle of Courage” is a philosophy of care that promotes belonging and healthy
relationships, teaches the mastery of skills, develops self-sufficiency and
independence, and encourages the virtue of generosity so that children can be
successful, contributing members of society.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

242

At the same time the Children’s Committee adopted this new
philosophy of care, we also revised the admissions criteria and developed
outcome measurements to track our success. The story of our children’s grade
point averages (gpa) says it all:
¾

Children in the program less than six months had an average
gpa of 1.46

¾

Children in the program six months to one year had an
average gpa of 2.47

¾

Children in the program over one year had an average gpa of
3.00

Another shift in our program is that instead of long-term placement, we
now emphasize family reunification whenever possible. To achieve this goal,
we actively engage the whole family in therapy. Since 2004, we have
successfully reunited 25 children with their families. To ensure their long-term
success, we currently serve 71 families through our Family Resource Center,
which provides counseling and other supportive services. We also assisted an
average of 12 families per month in accessing housing, food, furniture, public
social services, job placement and outside community mental health services.

Residential Services for Seniors
The Masonic Homes of California senior program continues to offer a
Home to our aging fraternal family. Services are provided on a first-come, firstserved basis to all eligible members regardless of financial status. We now have
a one to three year wait for services – depending on the level of care needed. I
am proud to report that our resident satisfaction scores place us among the top
retirement providers in the country and that our brethren are being served in a
manner in which we can all take pride.

Masonic Outreach Services (MOS)
Our MOS program has grown significantly over the past three years –
in fact we’ve seen a growth of over 35% over the past few years. This year we
completed the first-ever customer satisfaction for the program and achieved a
92% overall satisfaction rate. We are also working hard at improving our
communications and partnership with the lodges on caring for these vulnerable
members. The Masonic Homes of California is committed to never turning
away a widow or brother in need; however, this type of growth further strains

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

243

our charitable dollar and cannot be sustained without identifying new sources of
revenue.
FINANCIAL CHALLENGES
In 2000, 90% of the operating costs for the Homes were paid from our
interest earnings. During this same period, the market experienced a downturn,
which resulted in significant losses in our portfolio. This raised our awareness
that we could not tie our ability to provide charitable services to market
performance.
By focusing on improving the efficiency of our operations, now in
2006 we have reduced our reliance on investment earnings to 75% of our
operating income. However, we are still too heavily reliant on investment
earnings for healthy and sustainable operations. To put our performance in
perspective, the average non-profit in California derives no more than 10% of
their operating revenue from investments.
Further complicating this financial picture is the fact that the health
care inflationary rate is 2.5 times greater than standard inflation. In fact, our
current projections are that we will not be able to maintain our current level of
services, if we do not identify a new source of revenue by 2009.
Our
commitment to our historical mission and the need to develop a long-term,
sustainable financial strategy for our organization focused our strategic
planning.

SERVING OUR BRETHREN
The Board of Trustees has arrived at a concept for creating two new
Masonic senior living communities on both of our existing campuses. Both
campuses have a significant amount of land that currently lies fallow. Creating
new retirement communities on this land will create a revenue producing asset
for the Masonic Homes as well as a new source of service and security for our
middle-income constituents.
These new communities are both planned as Continuing Care
Retirement Communities (CCRCs), which means that they are active
communities for independent seniors and also provide more advanced levels of
care - should the need arise. The new communities have been named Acacia
Creek at Covina and Acacia Creek at Union City and admissions to these
communities at this time are offered to California Master Masons, their wives,
widows and mothers.
We have created two separate 501(c)3 corporations and have applied
for tax-exempt status to build and operate these new communities. Masonic
Homes of California is the sole corporate member of each Acacia entity and
elects the Acacia Creek boards, subject to approval by the Grand Master. These

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

244

separate corporations insulate the Masonic Homes by:
¾

Protecting and isolating the Endowment and General funds from
operations, liabilities and risks of Acacia Creeks.

¾

Protecting and preserving the current admission policies of
existing facilities.

Masonic Homes of California will always be open to all eligible
Masons – regardless of their financial assets. Because we cannot afford any
additional claims on our charitable dollars, Acacia Creek residents will have to
financially qualify for admission. Residents will pay a refundable or nonrefundable entrance fee and an ongoing monthly fee.

Development Process
We have designed a development process that would allow us to
minimize our investment while we tested the membership’s interest and demand
for such projects. As one Trustee aptly put it, “Our approach is not ‘if we build
it they will come – rather we will build it IF they come.” The development
process is structured in phases. Depending on the success of each phase, the
Board then votes to move forward or abandon the projects.
At this point, our sales pace far exceeds our expectations. This is
gratifying to the Board of Trustees for we are now assured that we are providing
a service that meets the membership’s needs. All of our initial planning and
research suggested these communities were needed and desired by our fraternity
– and the pace of our sales has affirmed these assumptions. If the membership
continues to respond so favorably, the Acacia Creek corporations look forward
to breaking ground in late 2007.
Through the new Acacia Creek corporations, an additional 580 elderly
members of our fraternal family will cared for in the manner reflective of our
ideals. In addition, the Acacia Creek projects will generate revenue for the
Masonic Homes of California through land leases and management fees. The
revenue generated would allow the Masonic Homes to care for an additional (on
an annual basis):
¾

87 seniors in residential services at the Masonic Homes OR

¾

99 children in homes from neglect and abuse OR

¾

309 seniors given the choice to remain in their own home and
home community, while being cared for by our Masonic Outreach
Services (MOS) department.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

245

Given that these projects allow us to put unused land to work for us –
and work for our fraternity – and that they fill an unmet need among our
constituents, while generating revenue to support the charitable mission of the
Masonic Homes, we are excited by our prospects. Should the membership
continue to respond so favorably, we look forward to 2009 when we stand
before two new communities built by and for our Masonic brethren.

COMMUNICATIONS
We provide services in the name, and on the behalf, of every Mason in
California as such it is important that our constituents stay connected to the
services we provide. To that end, this year the Masonic Homes of California
and both of the Acacia Creek corporations have invested considerable energy in
effectively communicating with our membership. This has included:
¾

Hosting an event for all the Past Grand Masters in which we
reviewed operations today and briefed them on the plans for
Acacia Creek at Union City and Acacia Creek at Covina.

¾

Conducting multiple informational sessions across the state
with our brethren

¾

Mailing posters on a monthly basis to every lodge in
California

¾

Monthly mailings and invitations to the membership

¾

Sending complete informational packets on the Masonic
Homes to our Entered Apprenticed Masons

¾

Revamping our Masonic Homes website and launched a new
website for the Acacia Creek projects

¾

Creating a new video on the Acacia Creek projects

Our goal in all of this is to ensure the Masonic Homes continues its
tradition of excellence and remains a place that every Mason in California can
be proud to call Home.

CONCLUSION
It is an honor and a privilege to a member of this Board. It is a
tremendous responsibility and one that I, and every Trustee, take very seriously.
I here want to thank each and every member of the Board of Trustees of the

246

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Masonic Homes of California for their heroic efforts on behalf of this great
institution. The discussions at our Board meetings are spirited and penetrating,
while we remain united in our purpose. In all that we do, we are guided by our
commitment to serving our brethren in a manner reflective of our principles. I
stand here today to assure you that we are ever focused and committed to
upholding a legacy of caring that extends more than a century. We will not,
because we cannot, fail.

Respectfully submitted,

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE VICE PRESIDENT
September 2005

The management team of the Masonic Homes of California is
comprised of the Executive Director, Union City, Robert Fallon, the Executive
Director, Covina, John Howl, and the Director of Program Development and
Masonic Outreach Services, Steffani Kizziar. Each of these managers report to
the Executive Vice President. In addition, the Homes contracts with Grand
Lodge Corporate Services to provide a variety of staff support services,
including Finance, Human Resources, Information Technology, Membership
Services and the Office of Philanthropy. The Chief Financial Officer of Grand
Lodge is Frank Tsai, who started in his position in July, 2006. The Director of
the Office of Philanthropy for Masonic Services is Doug Ismail. The Director
of Human Resources for the Grand Lodge/Homes is Linda Schomaker. The
Director of Information Technology is John Wickey. The corporate services
staff also functions as members of the Masonic Homes of California executive
team.
In 2006 John Howl assumed the responsibility for project oversight of
several strategic initiatives in addition to his operational role at Covina. His title
was changed to Vice President of Strategic Development for the Masonic
Homes of California.
The following sections highlight the achievements during the last year.
More detailed summaries of operational milestones are included in the reports of
the Executive Directors of the two campuses.

Acacia Creek Masonic Senior Living Communities
As a culmination of several years of analysis by the Board of Trustees
of Masonic Homes of California, the Board incorporated two subsidiary
corporations in 2006: Acacia Creek, A Masonic Senior Living Community in
Covina and Acacia Creek, A Masonic Senior Living Community in Union City.
The purpose of these communities is to broaden our services to serve members
of the fraternity at market- based entrance rates, utilizing land on both campuses
which currently lies fallow and generating an additional income stream to
support the underlying charitable mission of the Masonic Homes of California,
including our Outreach program. Development of the projects is being
conducted on a “phased-in” approach in order to minimize the capital costs
placed at risk and allowing the Board to gradually increase our funding
commitments as the degree of interest in the project is measured at several
points during development. In Covina the plans call for development of 171
independent living units, 16 assisted living units and 18 dementia units. In
Union City, 189 independent living units, 14 assisted living units and 16-20

248

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

dementia units are planned. The average size of the independent living units
range from 900 to 1500 square and the average two bedrooms, two bath
residence will be 1200 square feet.
The establishment of the two new corporations, for which we are
applying 501(c)3 tax exempt status to the Internal Revenue Service, was
enabled by the passage of two Grand Master Decisions at the 2005 annual
communication.
Operation of the two new enterprises under separate
subsidiaries from the Masonic Homes enables us to protect the Homes and its
assets from any liabilities associated with the expanded operation. At the time
of this writing, the level of interest on the part of the fraternity in the
development, as measured by the number of deposits placed on units, has been
exceedingly high.
A separate Board of Directors was established for both corporations
with Larry Adamson as President, Michael Neben as Vice President. The
remaining five members of the Acacia Creek Boards are Ara Maloyan, George
Geanoulis, Philip McAllister, Ken G. Nagel and Frank Loui.

Managing Resident Acuity and Waiting Lists
In 2006 we continued a second year of managing the acuity, or level of
care requirements, of the residents of our Homes by committing to a fixed ratio
of independent to assisted living residents. The policy of limiting the number of
assisted living admissions to the Homes has been vital in controlling the
naturally escalating costs of providing health care services in our facilities. We
have addressed the likelihood that many assisted living applicants on our
waiting lists might have to wait as long as 2 to 3 years for admission by
increasing resources committed to our Masonic Outreach Services (MOS)
program. The MOS staff collaborates closely with the Homes staff to determine
if assisted living applicants on the waiting list can be placed in high quality
community based facilities either as a temporary measure until space in the
Homes became available or as a permanent placement.
Through the creation of a centralized intake function, all inquiries for
admission or assistance are now quickly assessed and triaged so that their needs
can be met expediently. By working in partnership with preferred providers in
our communities, including board and care facilities, residential care facilities
for the elderly, skilled nursing facilities, and dementia or memory support
facilities, the Homes has been able to extend our mission to more recipients than
if we tried to provide all services within our own facilities.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

249

Masonic Outreach Services (MOS)
Our MOS program has experienced steady growth over the past three
years. In FY 2006, we averaged 105 clients receiving financial support each
month. Our focus this year has been on improving the partnership with Lodges
and the family of clients in providing care to these vulnerable seniors as well as
reducing our cost per case. In this regard, we have been successful and are
thankful to the Lodges and families for their increased participation. Through
their support and by maximizing state and federal benefits, we were able to
reduce the average cost per client per month from a budget of $1800 to an actual
of just over $1600.
We also began providing and tracking ongoing case management
services to those who needed help accessing services but did not need direct
financial assistance. Our case management only client load has averaged 10
clients per month.
This year we also developed and implemented a new software program
that allows us to track, monitor and report on cases as well as manage our
Centralized Intake function. The volume of daily inquiries coming into
centralized intake either relating to admission to the Homes or MOS support,
has increased substantially as a result of our communications initiatives with the
Lodges and concerted efforts to share information about the Acacia Creek
projects with the brethren. We view this as a very positive sign that there is
increased awareness of the services offered by the Homes.
Finally, the first MOS Customer Satisfaction Survey was developed
and implemented in 2006. Surveys were mailed to families, lodges and clients
asking for their feedback on services provided and suggestions for overall
improvement of our programs. We were gratified by the positive response and
achieved a 92% overall satisfaction with our programs and services.

Quality of Care
Evaluation of resident and applicant satisfaction with our services is an
important indicator to the Board and management of the effectiveness and
quality of our services. The Board of Trustees is also committed to the principal
that expansion of services through plans like the Acacia Creek communities,
must be based on demonstrated excellence in the services we currently provide.
For the second year in a row, we used an outside survey firm, Holleran
Consulting, to conduct our resident satisfaction surveys. Holleran is noteworthy
for maintaining some of the finest non-profit Continuous Care Retirement
Communities (CCRC’s) in the country among their clients. Our 2006
satisfaction results, in which a record number of residents participated in
completing a questionnaire, compare very favorably with Holleran’s national
data base of outstanding facilities and showed improvement over prior years in

250

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

many areas of service, including quality of food service, satisfaction with
recreation programs and satisfaction with spiritual support and chaplaincy
services. Management presents the results of satisfaction surveys to the Board
and the residents themselves, using the findings to plan our priorities for
continued enhancement of our programs and operations.
We are participating in two research projects with the University of
California San Francisco in an attempt to participate in adding to the academic
community’s body of knowledge about the aging process, care of the elderly
and thereby benefiting our own senior residents. One research project relates to
proper techniques of hand washing and its impact on disease prevention among
residents; the second study examines why seniors choose to participate or not
participate in recreational activity and exercise programs.
Our surveys of new residents regarding satisfaction with our admission
process showed an overall 94% satisfaction (94% in 2005) at Union City and
94% (up from 92%) at Covina. A new “user friendly” application allows our
fraternal family to apply either for MOS or admission using the same document.

Children’s Program
Following the development of a new strategic plan for our children’s
program and its approval by the Board last year, we have transitioned from the
Boy’s and Girl’s Town program to a new model of care that places more
emphasis on therapy and counseling in addition to the behavior modification
that was stressed in the past. We have set goals for increased family
participation in counseling and therapy and reunification of children with their
families when the family dynamics permit. Family reunification rates have
increased and we place emphasis on follow-up with program alumni to ensure a
successful reunification and development of the life skills necessary to make a
successful transition outside the Home. We plan to track this data, including the
academic and job success of our alumni as a measurement of the effectiveness
of the program. In addition a new outreach program called the Family Resource
Center has added about 50 additional clients and families followed through the
Children’s Program on an outpatient basis.

Employee Relations and Organizational Development
In health care organizations, our workforce is our most important asset.
Over 50% of our expenses are labor related. Given the challenges most long
term care providers have in employee recruitment and retention, we are proud
that our employee turnover rates are remarkably low. Position descriptions have
been updated and workplace safety audits have been completed at both
campuses, including ergonomics evaluation and work station redesign.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

251

While we need to be a competitive employer in the design of our wage
and benefit packages, we also know successful employee relations rest with
effective and prepared managers and clear-cut expectations and performance
standards for employees. We have invested time in developing the skills of our
managers in problem resolution, communication and effective supervision. Our
Human Resources Department has arranged for individualized coaching for
senior management to refine their management and leadership skills.
We know that our employees value their benefit packages, which in
our case are quite competitive in our industry, as much or more than their
compensation levels. Following the conversion last year to a paid time off
system that affords employees more flexibility in scheduling their time off while
reducing potential misuse of sick time. This year we modified our employee
premium structure to incorporate a shared responsibility with employees for
their health care premiums as a partial measure to offset rapidly increasing
employee health care costs.
Overall employee satisfaction surveys confirmed an overall satisfaction
of 82% which compares favorably to an external industry benchmark of 79%
satisfaction.

Financial results
For the past several years, the Board and management have striven to
keep our operational expenses within 5% of the past 5-year averages of the
balance of our investment portfolio. Like many non-profit organizations, we
feel this kind of prudent usage of our investment proceeds ensures that we will
have adequate resources to care for our current as well as future residents and
clients, especially in light of the aging demographics of our membership.
We were very pleased that for several years in a row, we not only
produced a budget that fell below the 5% spending target, but we achieved a
$1,900,000 favorable variance in net expenses compared to the budget. Each
year we have saved the favorable variances between the targeted 5% and actual
net spending in a strategic reserve fund. The strategic reserve fund will then
provide seed capitalization in the form of loans to finance some of the key
strategic initiatives described in the President’s report and other committee
reports. As of June 30, 2006, the balance of the Strategic Reserve Fund was
$5,264,557. Our capitalization strategy ensures that our new strategic
initiatives, which will of course themselves contribute to the extension of our
charitable mission, will in no way be financed out of endowment funds.
A key mechanism for keeping our spending below budgeted levels has
been on-going scrutiny of costs, exploration of system improvement
opportunities and maximization of outside revenue sources. We continue to
compare our costs for various elements of services (e.g. food costs per day, or

252

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

nursing care costs per day) compare favorably with available industry
benchmarks. In addition, in an effort to offset the dramatic increase in the
number of MOS clients served, we were able to reduce the cost of care for each
MOS client by over 30% through careful management of our client’s budgets
and accessing available reimbursement.
This year we completed an analysis of the projected life and
replacement needs for all our capital resources. This capital reserve analysis
will allow us to project our long-term capital needs, which is then incorporated
annually into our budget. We also set aside the full cash value of our
depreciation each year into a Capital Reserve account, in order to fund future
capital needs. This is a very fiscally prudent practice which is not able to be
accomplished in many health care organizations. The balance of our capital
reserve account as of June 30, 2006 was $14,995,135.

Strategic Initiatives:
Summaries of the key strategic initiatives pursued by management over
the last year are included in the report of the President and the Strategic
Planning Committee.
In closing, the management team wants to thank and acknowledge our
outstanding members of the Board of Trustees, who give countless hours of
their time and creative thinking for the benefit of our residents, clients and
fraternal constituents.
Respectfully submitted,
Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Foundation continues to expand its role to provide outreach to the
youth of California and our public education system, as desired by the Brethren
of our Jurisdiction. The connection between the California Masonic Memorial
Temple Board of Trustees and the Foundation Board of Trustees as reported last
year, continues as an integral linkage in our overall philanthropic structure.
The Foundation serves our youth in a number of ways:
•

This year, 44 new and 36 renewal scholarships were awarded to
deserving high school graduating seniors and college students to
pursue undergraduate degrees. The total awarded directly from the
Foundation was $167, 500. The C.E. Towne scholarship fund,
managed by Wells Fargo Bank but awarded by the Foundation,
provided 54 renewal scholarships with a value of $540,000.

•

The Foundation, with assistance of the Masonic Homes of
California, continues to provide resident 3-day Masonic Student
Assistance Program workshops to public school educators, along
with 1-day booster sessions. Since our jurisdiction began this
program in 1994, 4,500 teachers have been trained, and over
44,000 students have been referred. It should be noted that to
expand the providing of this valuable education, the Foundation is
preparing guidelines for local Lodges to participate with school
districts in holding these training sessions. Naval Lodge No. 87 of
Vallejo is planning their second such event to be held in 2007.
Additionally, the State Department of Education is recognizing the
value of this training, and we expect more use of our program
training within our state.

•

Supporting the California Teacher of the Year program is another
important outreach from the Foundation. By awarding $1000 to
each of the five teachers selected from across the state, every
Mason in our state is held in esteem. These awards are presented
by the Foundation to the teachers, in their own community, and
attendance by the local Lodge is encouraged. It should be noted
that each of these honorees never forget the award from the
Masons of California.

•

The Child I.D. program, with funding from the Foundation,
continues to grow in both number of events, and children
recorded. This year at the State Fair, and 10 county fairs, over
23,000 children were identified. Many of our local Lodges have

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

254

embraced this program as part of their community outreach, and
this valuable service continues in demand, in towns large and
small.
•

Continuing assistance for Leadership Training in each of our youth
groups through financial assistance.

•

By grants of $1,000 to each school that sponsors a Cornerstone
ceremony by our Grand Lodge officers. This is a very public
display of the commitment to public education by our Craft, and
highlights the local Lodge to all in attendance.

The Foundation also provides for education of our membership, and
training for our officers. The annual giving to the Foundation by our Brethren
supports:
•

The Wardens Leadership Retreats, an important training tool for
our Lodge officer cadre.

•

The annual Masonic Symposium, a premier Masonic research
event.

•

Masonic Education materials to further the knowledge and
understanding of our Masonic way of life.

Finally, the Foundation continues to oversee the preservation of not
only the California Masonic Memorial Temple, but the numerous historic sites,
located around the state, that are owned by the Grand Lodge. An important
undertaking now underway is the project to restore and perpetually maintain the
wonderful endomosaic mural in the lobby of our Nob Hill building. It is vitally
important to preserve this visual story of Masonry in California for generations
to come.
The Trustees of the Foundation work diligently all year to represent our
constituents, and I personally thank them for their dedication. To you, Most
Worshipful, we thank you for the opportunity to serve our gentle Craft.
Respectfully submitted,

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple are pleased
to present the 51st annual report to the Grand Lodge on issues concerning the
condition and management of the physical facility as well as the financial
condition of our Memorial Temple. This report is an overview and abstract of
activities this year. Those wanting or needing more detailed information are
referred to the minutes of the quarterly meetings of the Board of Trustees filed
in the Grand Lodge office.
Full and complete audited financials are not included in this report.
Those wishing to may review these records which are included in the
appendices of these proceedings.
Execution of our five year plan is on schedule, especially with the
completion of upgrading the main elevators. Following the theft of the Grand
Lodge Jewels your board authorized Ligouri and Associates to survey, study,
and recommend a security system that would function 24/7. The cost of this
program imposed a significant increase in the annual operating budget of the
building, and your board believes that the gradually increasing revenue
generated from event contractors justifies the assumption of this ongoing
expense. It was important to mention this, because the insurance company has
made a reasonable settlement with the Grand Lodge. The executive committee
will determine the best use of these funds.
Frank Tsai the new Chief Financial Officer of the Grand Lodge of Free
and Accepted Masons of California began his official duties on July 5th. His
expertise will be of great value in working with all of our financial and
budgetary issues. He brings a wealth of experience in the business/financial
world to us and is no stranger to the position of CFO.
On the advice of our accounting firm and tax attorneys the board has
authorized the re-establishment of a for profit corporation whose name will be
Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. This decision was made to comply with IRS
rules. The CMMT will continue as a support organization of the California
Masonic Foundation (CMF) and will lease the building and parking facility to
this newly formed corporation.
Your board approved a resolution to provide temporary relocation of
residents of the Masonic Home in Union City in the event of an emergency.
This action was previously approved by your board, and it was felt that an
official resolution should be adopted.

In June, we received notification from the Society of National
Association Publications (SNAP) that the California Freemason magazine was
awarded national honors in the SNAP 2006 Excel Awards.
SNAP is an association of more than 750 U.S. membership
organizations that publish magazines and newsletters. These organizations
represent a wide variety of trade and professional associations, charities, and
academic, cultural, and service organizations. Members include the American
Bar Association, Habitat for Humanity, the American Diabetes Association;
Jewish Women International, Rotary International, U.S. Golf Association,
Sigma Xi, and Penn State Alumni Association.

publishing. The top three publications are selected in several categories and

Olympics.

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON CODE REVISION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
The Craft, at the 2005 Annual Communication, adopted the Grand
Master’s Recommendation to appoint a special Code Revision Committee. The
committee was asked to undertake a comprehensive review of the California
Masonic Code for inconsistencies and mistakes which have accumulated over
time and to make recommendations for appropriate corrections and updates.

262

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

It is helpful to understand the history of our Code when considering a
revision of it. In the early years of our Grand Lodge, the written Masonic law of
this jurisdiction was contained in a Constitution, a set of Bylaws which provided
certain procedural rules for Grand Lodge communications and a series of
standing resolutions which were adopted by the craft over the years to resolve
issues which had arisen under the Constitution or Bylaws or to address issues
where the Constitution and Bylaws were silent. These standing resolutions
eventually became the Ordinances in our current Code.
While our Code has been revised in 1956, 1975 and 1991, the same
organization of the Code continued: a Constitution with a series of special rules
and exceptions in the Ordinances.
Other than the adoption of new
commercialism rules in 1995, there has been no significant attempt to put all
rules on the same subject in the same area of the Code nor to address issues
which are not covered by the Code. There are many unwritten rules followed
by custom in this jurisdiction which have never found their way into the Code.
Your committee was asked by Grand Master Sorsabal to produce a
draft restatement of the Code to address these deficiencies. As is now the
general state practice for corporations, we propose a Constitution which looks
like a set of corporate Articles of Incorporation: short with only the most
pertinent rules of the organization included. All other rules are proposed for the
Ordinances. This reorganization allowed the committee to place rules
governing the same subject in one place and to eliminate duplications found of
the same rule in different parts of the Code. The Ordinances have been
reorganized in an order which generally follows the order found in corporate
Bylaws. Within topic headings, rules have been included in the order in which
the process addressed would be undertaken. Previously unwritten rules
followed by custom have been included, and we have clarified existing rules
where custom has not been consistent with the prior published Code. We have
included cross references. We have attempted to simplify complicated
language, although as many of us on the committee are practicing or retired
lawyers, simplicity to us may not be simplicity to you.
We have attempted to avoid changing the law in doing this restatement,
although because lawyers can argue over every comma it is impossible to say
that the Code proposed may not on occasion produce a different result than
would have been the case under our current Code. However, we do not believe
that there have been any material changes, except as follows. We have
eliminated some provisions which are obsolete or should be dealt with
somewhere other than in the Code. (The deleted provisions are set forth in the
Appendix to our report which was published last June.)
There are
inconsistencies in the current Code, and we have attempted to retain what we
felt were best practices. We have updated portions of the Code which were not
consistent with state law, such as Grand Lodge’s relationship with the Masonic
Homes, California Masonic Foundation and California Masonic Memorial

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

263

Temple. There are some revisions in trial procedures, including the addition of
a burden of proof. We have added definitions of ritual, moral turpitude and
what constitutes the law in this Jurisdiction.
We believe that the result of our efforts is a draft Code which is easier
to read, addresses more issues and hopefully will reduce the number of future
disagreements as to what the Code requires.
The appendix and source information for Code provisions are
presented to assist in the review of our proposal but are not part of the Code.
The table of contents, index and cross-reference tables are not part of the Code
and will be prepared after the Code is adopted.
We have just heard from Policy that this new Code was prepared in
haste and has numerous errors, although no errors are listed in their report.
Policy’s report came as a great surprise to many of us because we did not learn
of these objections until yesterday morning.
Great care was taken in the process which resulted in the Code before
you. When the current Blue Code was proposed for adoption, Policy also
recommended that it be rejected because they did not have time to review it. To
avoid a similar result this time, Policy was included in the writing of the new
Code right from the start. The Chairman of Policy was a member of the Code
Revision Committee from the very beginning. He saw all drafts and made a
number of helpful comments. Never once was it suggested that our work
product contained errors or was being done in haste. He had the first draft of
the revised Code on January 2nd of this year and every draft thereafter.
The Executive Committee has had the draft of the new Code since
January 2nd. All of the boards and major committees of the Grand Lodge have
had the draft of the new Code since the first of May. The Committee on Ritual
reviewed the draft, suggested some changes to the definition of Ritual, and those
changes were made. Trial Review suggested some changes, but the Code
Revision Committee felt that these changes were not consistent with current law
and should be proposed by separate legislation. The chair of Policy suggested
changes, and these were addressed. We received no other suggested changes or
reports of errors. The proposed Code was published on the Grand Lodge public
web site the first of June. We have received no reports of errors as a result.
Indeed, since the proposed Code has been released, I have used the
new Code in responding to questions posed to me as chair of Jurisprudence,
because the new Code is so much easier to use! I then use the cross references
to document my opinion with what is in the current Code. I have therefore used
this new Code a great deal, and I have found no errors. This new Code is
principally the work of Most Worshipful Jack Levitt and me. We have between
us over thirty years of experience on Jurisprudence, thirteen of them as chair.
Jack chaired the 1991 revision committee. Most Worshipful Don McCaw has
also chaired Jurisprudence, reviewed the proposed Code and reported no errors.

264

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We think that the work product before you was carefully drawn and has been
fully vetted by all the key leaders in Grand Lodge. Jack and I, along with a
number of other key leaders of our fraternity, believe that the Code is ready for
your consideration in its current form.
We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity which he has given us
to serve in this important assignment.
Respectfully submitted,
R. Stephen Doan, PGM, Chairman
Jack R. Levitt, PGM
Ralph B. Dash

Richard A. Meyers
Maxwell S. “Mickey” Roth
John L. Cooper III, Grand Secretary

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON JURISPRUDENCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to the Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to
carry out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it
comments on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and
on the legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolutions. After the
Annual Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code
to reflect the legislation which was adopted.
In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Sorsabal’s decisions
and dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion.
In the Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July
21st of this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents, and the
legislation contained in Grand Master Sorsabal’s recommendations which he
will present in his message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that
each piece of this legislation has the legal effect set forth in it.
The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 21st of this year
and the legislation contained in Grand Master Sorsabal’s recommendations
require no separate motion because they are automatically before this Annual
Communication. The presiding officer will announce the vote required for the
passage of each item before discussion begins. Any legislation which does not
amend the California Masonic Code or the Ritual requires a simple majority
only for adoption. Any amendment to the California Masonic Code or the
Ritual must be adopted by a five-sixths affirmative vote. If the amendment

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

265

receives a simple majority but less than a five-sixths affirmative vote, it carries
over to the next Annual Communication when only a two-thirds affirmative vote
is required for adoption.
We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity which he has given us
to serve this year.
Respectfully submitted,
R. Stephen Doan, P.G.M., Chairman
Michael D. Castelli
Jack R. Levitt, P.G.M.
Lester W. Miller
Richard L. Riemer

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON LEADERSHIP DEVELOPMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
Leadership Development is one of the key priorities outlined in the
Grand Lodge Strategic Plan released earlier this year. The Leadership
Development Committee has taken aggressive and dedicated steps to align our
efforts with this strategic plan.
Nearly 1,000 people participated in the leadership training programs
offered by the Grand Lodge in this fiscal year. The training programs include
the Wardens’ Leadership Retreats, Lodge Management Certification Programs,
and Inspectors’ Seminars.

Wardens’ Leadership Retreats
The focus of the Wardens’ Leadership Retreats is to prepare lodge
wardens to be leaders of Masonry in their community. Through a two-part
strategic planning program divided between the Junior and Senior Warden
Retreats, wardens learn to develop a vision for their lodge, goals and objectives
to meet that vision, and a financial plan to support the strategic initiatives of the
lodge. Time is also devoted to sharing ideas about lodge programming in the
areas of membership development, Masonic education, and community
involvement. The wardens are encouraged to bring their spouses and a special
training program is provided for them, in addition to their participation with the
wardens on certain subjects.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

266

Lodge Management Certification Program
The Lodge Management Certification Program offers in-depth training
on some of the most fundamental lodge operations. The program covers five
subjects: Lodge Finance, Program Planning, Hall Association Management,
Membership Development, and Lodge Administration. Each subject has four
courses and the training takes one entire day. After completing that four course
in one of the above subjects, attendees are given an exam on the material.
Those who pass the exam are given a certificate in that subject.

Inspectors’ Seminars
These seminars include a four hour course for new Inspectors on the
eve of a full day of training for all Inspectors. The program includes training
and discussion on the overall leadership training strategy of the Grand Lodge,
technological resources, lodge administration and finance. Inspectors consider
ideas on how to be most effective in their leadership role.

ON MEMBERSHIP DEVELOPMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
My name is Gary L. Peterson, and I am a Past Master of Saddleback
Laguna Lodge No 672, and Chairman of the Membership Development
Committee.
It is my pleasure to present to you the Annual Report of the
Membership Development Committee for the 2006 Masonic year.
I would first like to acknowledge and thank the committeemen and
Grand Lodge staff members who throughout this year have continued to
contribute their time and energy in supporting the development of this
Committee’s successful programs. Those committee members are Wor. Gary L.
Peterson, Past Master of Saddleback Laguna Lodge No 672; Wor. James E.
Banta, Past Master of Fresno Lodge No 247; Wor. Salvatore A. Sciortino, Past
Master of Wisdom Lodge No 202; Wor Anil Comelo, Master of Navel Lodge
No 87; Wor Stephen Hanover, Past Master of Trinity Lodge No 27 ; Wor Ricky
Keltner, PM, Union Kit Carson Lodge No 58; Wor Frederick Votto, Master of
Liberal Arts Lodge No 677; Brother Erik Borg, a member of SaddlebackLaguna No 682; and Wor. Edward Avetisian, Past Master of Burbank Lodge No
406. The Grand Lodge staff members are Wor. Allan L. Casalou, Assistant
Grand Secretary and a Past Master of Acalanes-Fellowship Lodge No. 480;
Brother Angel Alvarez-Mapp, Communications Manager and a member of

268

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Acalanes-Fellowship Lodge No 480, and Mr. Jeff Reyes, Administrative
Assistant.
At your direction, our committee has been charged with the
development and implementation of a “Comprehensive Membership Plan” that
would successfully address the issue of increasing our membership levels by
attracting new leaders to our Fraternity. This charge resulted in the successful
“Pass It on” membership program, now in its fourth year.
Again this year, “Pass It On” presentations were made at the Junior
Warden’s retreats, and the Lodge Management Certification Programs.
Additionally, committee members continued the systematic process of making
“Pass it On” presentations to individual lodges and District officers meetings
throughout the state, and the annual Grand Lodge Secretary’s Workshop. The
Committee will also again have an informational booth during Grand Lodge.
The Committee is changing its future direction of “Pass It On”
involvement from the instructional presentation format that has been in use, to a
more direct, “coaching like”, approach with a maximum of 50 lodges with each
Committeeman coaching approximately five individual lodges. Determining the
criteria for lodge selection, marketing, and job duties are currently projects. The
idea is to help the lodges help themselves via multi-media, phone, and e-mail.
Much of what is learned through this method may also be applicable to any
lodge rescue efforts that may in future occur.
I am pleased to report to you that we again set a record for new
membership within our Jurisdiction. The year end shows 1,223 new men raised
as Master Masons with many lodges raising over our goal of five per lodge.
This data reflects a growth in new Masons that we believe should continue.
Grand Lodges new “On The Level” Masonic information Program has
also been developed and completed for distribution this year. This new tool will
greatly enhance a Lodge’s ability to reach out to the community in a clear and
concise manner when telling them about our fraternity. “On The Level”
contains CD’s, instructional manuals, marketing samples, and scripts that go
along with the informative power point presentation.
At the request of Most Worshipful Sorsabal, the Committee has
designed a Certificate of excellence for presentation to each lodge that attains
the “Pass It On” goal of five new Master Masons raised during the year. In
addition a traveling plaque will be available to the lodge which raised the most
Master Masons.
The MDC will continue to evaluate its programs as to overall
effectiveness of the individual components. Necessary adjustments will
continue to be made as the program implementation continues.

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
TRIAL REVIEW
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Since the 2005 Annual Communication your Committee on Trial
Review has reviewed 28 completed cases, of which 3 were resolved without
trial. There are 15 matters pending trial. The Committee has met with the Grand
Secretary’s assistant, Pamela Lindsten, on an average of 3 days per month to
correct procedural errors, to identify and expedite the appointment of trial
masters, and to assist the Grand Master in these matters when needed. We
would like to acknowledge the extraordinary support and cooperation of the
Very Worshipful John L. Cooper and Pamela Lindsten and Albert Donnici.
The Committee has reviewed each of the trials numbered 2453, 2466,
2484, 2485, 2486, 2487, 2488, 2489, 2490, 2491, 2493, 2494, 2495, 2501,
2502, 2503, 2504, 2505, and 2506, and finds that in each case there was
sufficient compliance with the procedural requirements of our Constitution and
Ordinances; that the findings and conclusions are supported by the trial record;
and, that there was no miscarriage of justice.
Your Committee therefore submits and moves for adoption the
following resolution:
“Resolved that in each of the trials numbered above the judgment and
penalty imposed be and are ratified, affirmed and approved.”
Your Committee feels that in each of the following trials a special
commentary must be stated:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

270

Trial Records 2496, 2497, 2498, 2499 and 2500
In each of these cases, the unmasonic conduct of the individuals
involved extraordinary efforts to conceal the theft of lodge funds, and to protect
the perpetrator.
The Committee recommends that the penalty be increased to expulsion,
and therefore submits and moves for adoption of the following resolution:
“Resolved that in each of the trials numbered above the penalty be
expulsion.”

Trial Record 2492
Because the unmasonic conduct in this matter involved child
molestation, and was freely admitted by the accused, this committee
recommends that the penalty be increased to expulsion, and therefore submits
and moves for adoption the following resolution:
“Resolved that in the matter of Trial Record 2492 the penalty be
expulsion.”

Respectfully submitted,
Richard A. Meyers, Chairman
David D. Goble
Thomas C. Letcher

Maxwell S. Roth
Robert E. Scott
Donald R. Fretz, Consultant

NAVIGATION TIPS

Scroll through the document using
scroll bars on the side of the window or
the page navigation tool at the bottom.

Click on any of the items in the bookmark
section of the document and you’ll navigate
directly to that page or section.
You can then move to the next page using the
ARROW KEYS on your keyboard.
Return to the beginning of the document at any
time by clicking on the Table of Contents
Bookmark.
If you want to enlarge or reduce the size of the
document, click on the Zoom In/Zoom Out
bookmarks.
Navigate through the document
by clicking on any of the items in
the Table of Contents or the
Index.
The “Hand Tool” will change to
an “Index Finger”…click on the
line and you’ll move to the
desired page.
To get back, click on the Table
of Contents/Index bookmarks.
